   1| 3
   2| IN MEMORIAM
   3| Sarah M. Grant passed away on February 4, 2022. She had lived in Lunenburg since
   4| 2012 and been a member of the Council on Aging from 2013 to 2022.
   5| Sarah founded one of the Council on Aging’s annual events, the Tea Party, which was a very
   6| popular event. She was very focused on all the little details that go into a formal tea party.
   7| Sarah also led craft classes at the Senior Center as she was very talented. She will be missed.
   8| William J. Gustus passed away in his home on September 19, 2022, surrounded by
   9| his loving family. Bill owned and operated Settlers Crossing Golf Course in Lunenburg
  10| and the Westminster Golf & Country Club in Westminster with his wife Laura Caron-
  11| Gustus and business partner Donnie Lyons. Prior to his retirement he was the Town
  12| Administrator for the Town of Lynnfield, and the Chief Administrative and Financial
  13| Officer for the Town of Lunenburg.
  14| Longtime dance and physical education teacher, Genevieve C. Nass, 67, died
  15| Nov. 29, 2022.  Genevieve was born on Sept. 30, 1955, attended Lunenburg
  16| schools and graduated from Lunenburg High School in 1973.
  17| Known for the twinkle in her eye and her big smile, she ran her own dance studio
  18| for more than 40 years and taught physical education for 20 years in the
  19| Lunenburg Public Schools.
  20| She looked forward to summers at the Town Beach and also directed the
  21| Lunenburg Town Beach for many years, acting as a lifeguard, swimming
  22| instructor and lifeguard trainer.

===PAGE 6=== (layout, 25 lines)
   1|                                        LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                        YEAR INCORPORATED-1728
   3|                                        REGISTERED VOTERS– 9041
   4|                                           FY’22 TAX RATE- $17.19
   5|                  OPEN TOWN GOVERNMENT– 5 MEMBER SELECT BOARD
   6|                     ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION– 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                      ANNUAL TOWN MEETING– 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   8|                                         TOTAL AREA– 26.63 MILES
   9|  Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4
  10|  square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts  approximately  43 miles from
  11|  Boston,  It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the
  12|  Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley and on the north by
  13|  the Town of Townsend.
  14|  Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Lake Whalom and the
  15|  former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This
  16|  district was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of
  17|  Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect
  18|  characteristics of these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills Lake area was
  19|  developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. Shirley Reservoir is a public
  20|  water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been modified to year-round
  21|  residences.
  22|                                                                                   Lunenburg
  23| Visit our website at https://www.lunenburgma.gov
  24| Follow us on social media on Facebook at https://www.facebook.com/lunenburgma
  25|                                                            6

===PAGE 18=== (layout, 53 lines)
   1|                                          REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                                                                FY22
   3| It is my pleasure to submit the annual report on the activities of the Office of the Town Manager during
   4| Fiscal Year 2022. Fiscal Year 2022 started off like most other years however, there were lingering after
   5| effects of the pandemic. The pandemic brought about a shift in the employment marketplace, causing
   6| vacancies to last longer and a smaller applicant pool to fill positions. The State extended the allowance of
   7| virtual public meetings, which most boards and committees continued to use as a matter of convenience and,
   8| at times, for personal health reasons. The federal stimulus funds that were part of the American Rescue Plan
   9| Act made their way down to cities and towns and this would result in many discussions in how best to use
  10| these windfall monies. Driver shortages, supply chain issues, and increased demand for items disrupted our
  11| Pay As You Throw program, severely impacted the delivery schedule of vehicles/equipment the Department
  12| of Public Works, Fire Department, and Police Department ordered and spiked costs for construction projects.
  13| Despite the ripple effects of the pandemic, the Town continued to provide a high level of services and was
  14| another year of progress. Below I would like to highlight the topics that were addressed and the
  15| accomplishments within the Office of the Town Manager:
  16|    In July of 2021, as part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed and voted to accept the
  17|     Town Manager’s annual goals for FY22. The Town Manager’s goals for FY22 included continuing to
  18|     take the necessary steps towards the redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, working with
  19|     the Select Board on creating a means tested senior citizen tax relief program, and action items related to
  20|     enhancing facilities maintenance and management.  A majority of the action items related to these three
  21|     goals were completed.  In regards to 925 Massachusetts Avenue, the Town Manager continued to work
  22|     Joe McLaughlin of BETA Group, MassDEP, and MassDevelopment about possible cleanup funding. In
  23|     the end we worked with the EPA and this project was put on the list of projects in line for federal funding
  24|     through Targeted Brownfields Assessment funding. The funding would pay for removal of the
  25|     underground tanks, limited soil removal and make the Town eligible for future cleanup funds. This work
  26|     is slated to be completed in FY 2023. In addition, a survey was completed for the property and after
  27|     much due diligence to eliminate any possibility that the Town would be legally responsible for cleanup,
  28|     the Town foreclosed on the property, which was a necessary step to receive the federal funding from the
  29|     EPA.As it related to one of the action items under the Town Manager’s annual goals for Facilities, the
  30|     Town Manager followed the Town Charter process for an Administrative Organization Plan and after a
  31|     public hearing process, received approval at the May 2022 Annual Town Meeting to separate Facilities
  32|     from the Department of Public Works. The third goal, to assist the Select Board with a means tested
  33|     senior citizen tax relief program was initiated and the Principal Assessor presented information from
  34|     other communities with similar programs to the working group. Ultimately additional time was needed to
  35|     develop the framework of the program and its effects on taxpayers, so this goal was carried forward to
  36|     FY 2023.
  37|    In July 2021, the Select Board set their goals for the upcoming year: they amended their FY21 goal/work
  38|     on the old Primary School at 30 School Street with additional action items such as engaging with a
  39|     landscape architecture firm to discuss a vision of multi-use, passive recreation/ green space project at the
  40|     site and all necessary steps to bring the associated costs to the 2022 Annual Town Meeting. Another goal
  41|     was to work with the Town Manager and Finance Director to establish a means-tested senior citizen
  42|     property tax exemption or abatement. The last goal was to develop a policy designed to encourage
  43|     volunteerism in Town, specifically for town boards, committees, and commissions. A Request for
  44|     Proposals was put out twice for selection of a landscape architect for 30 School Street and ultimately
  45|     architect Ray Dunetz was chosen for the project. Various staff and community meetings led to a
  46|     conceptual design that requested additional funding at the May 2022 Annual Town Meeting, but funding
  47|     was only approved for abatement and demolition of the structure and the associated engineering costs.
  48|     The goal of developing a policy designed to encourage volunteerism was discussed at a workshop but the
  49|     Board was split on the language of the policy so no action was taken during fiscal year 2022 on this goal.
  50|    At the end of Fiscal Year 2021 the Town received a letter from Mass Housing  that Pond View
  51|     Commons, LLC had filed an application for a 40B housing project. The proposed development would
  52|     consist of 200 units of rental housing on 18.5 acres of land located at 318, 390 & 400 Howard Street.
  53|                                                                  18

===PAGE 19=== (layout, 54 lines)
   1|    August 2022 began with a workshop on Chapter 40B led by Town Counsel Adam Costa on the role of the
   2|     Zoning Board of Appeals would provide in the application process. The Town was required to submit a
   3|     response to the letter from Mass Housing by September 2, 2021, which an 88-page response was
   4|     submitted that included numerous letters from residents, town boards, town counsel, and the Town
   5|     Manager. Mass Housing reviewed the town’s response, requested information from the applicant and
   6|     ultimately granted a positive 40B project eligibility in March of 2022. From that point on, the applicant
   7|     filed a comprehensive permit application with the Zoning Board of Appeals. The application is still
   8|     ongoing as of writing this report.
   9|    The Town Manager submitted recommendations to the Select Board on adoption or amendment of
  10|     various policies throughout the year, including the Capital Budget Policy changing the threshold for
  11|     individual projects from $10,000 to $20,000, a new Senior Citizen Property Work Off Program Policy
  12|     which  would  change  to  an  internal  function  of  reviewing  the  applications  by  the  Assistant  Town
  13|     Manager versus a committee, amendments to the Liquor License Regulations, the Boot Drive Policy,
  14|     MA Emergency Paid Sick Leave, and Sign Policy. In April the Board finalized a new Commemorative
  15|     Policy and began working on their third goal of a Volunteerism Policy. In May a new Tailings Policy,
  16|     which addresses uncashed checks issued by the Town, was adopted and in June a new Ambulance
  17|     Billing Collection Policy was adopted.
  18|    As previously mentioned, Fiscal Year 2022 was a difficult year for retaining staff and filling vacancies.
  19|     The pandemic created opportunities for employees to move to new careers and made it a more competitive
  20|     marketplace for employers. The Assistant Town Manager/HR Director who is responsible for advertising
  21|     vacancies and working with the relevant departments to bring on new employees was constantly reviewing
  22|     applications, interviewing and trying to fill vacancies throughout the year. In August 2021 the Veterans
  23|     Agent, IT Director (shared between Town/School), and a Board Clerk resigned. The Veterans Agent
  24|     position was filled in September, the Town Manager hired an outside consultant in September to fill the
  25|     role of the IT Director, and additional Board Clerks were hired throughout the year. In September
  26|     Principal Assessor Sue Byrne announced her retirement effective end of September and Christine Kumar
  27|     was hired in September to fill this position, but resigned at the end of November. The position was filled
  28|     with a temporary Assessor, Rena Swezey, from December 2021 until March of 2022 when Christopher
  29|     Carroll was selected to fill the permanent position. In October the Principal Clerk for the Town
  30|     Accountant retired and this position changed to an Assistant Town Accountant position after the
  31|     November Special Town Meeting and was filled in January 2022. Also in October the Recreation Director
  32|     Kayla Wright resigned and Rob Thompson was hired in December to fill this position. In March DPW
  33|     Director Rob Oliva resigned and this position was unfilled until May of 2022 when Bill Bernard was
  34|     appointed. Also in May of 2022 Conservation Administrator Matt Marro retired and Nadia Madden was
  35|     hired in May to fill this position. The Police Department also saw staffing changes with three Reserve and
  36|     Full-Time Officers leaving for other positions, and these were filled at different times during the year. The
  37|     DPW had up to three vacant Heavy Equipment Operator positions at all times during the year. The
  38|     Council on Aging and Public Access also had vacancies for Van Drivers and Videographers during the
  39|     fiscal year as well. The positions that were new to the FY22 budget, including the Assistant to the Sewer
  40|     Business Manager position, Board/Committee Clerk positions, Remote Meeting Coordinator, Trash &
  41|     Recycling Coordinator, an additional Police Officer, an additional Firefighter/Paramedic, and restoration
  42|     of a Facilities Technician position, were all filled at various times during the year.
  43|    The Town Manager, the Assistant Town Manager, and the relevant staff worked together on putting a
  44|     variety of projects out to bid in FY 2022, including the Landscape Architect Services for 30 School
  45|     Street (put out to bid twice due to no response on the first bid), Master Planning Services (no response to
  46|     bid), Master Planning/Landscape Architectural Services for Marshall Park, the Public Safety Building
  47|     Chiller project, Town Building Cleaning Services, Town Beach Building Abatement work, Town Beach
  48|     Building Alterations (bids came in over budget), DPW Road Materials, Library LED Lighting, 30 School
  49|     Street Building Abatement work, and LED Streetlight Design and Project Management Services (all bids
  50|     rejected).
  51|    This year was another productive year for infrastructure projects in Lunenburg. Through a $260,000
  52|     grant  the Town Manager received through the Complete Streets  grant program,  sidewalk  work on
  53|     Memorial Drive, the intersection of Main and Oak Avenue, and the sidewalks connecting Town Hall and
  54|                                                                  19

===PAGE 20=== (layout, 45 lines)
   1| and the sidewalks connecting Town Hall and then over upper common in front of 1 Main Street was
   2| completed. The front decking at the entrance of Town Hall was also replaced. Electrical work was done in the
   3| Town Hall basement to address bringing certain wiring up to code and to eliminate unnecessary old wiring.
   4| The environmental consultant hired for the 104 Fairview Road project (also known as the Woodruff Property)
   5| filed a Notice of Intent with the Conservation Commission and the Town was issued an Order of Conditions
   6| in order to proceed with putting this work out to bid. The Pleasant Street Bridge project, which was funded
   7| through a Small Bridge Grant, was completed by the fall of 2021 and the MassDOT bridge project on Mass.
   8| Avenue was completed later that fiscal year. Through a $50,000 state earmark, hazardous abatement work
   9| was undertaken at the old Primary School at 30 School Street. Hazardous material abatement was also
  10| completed at the Town Beach building. The Town also began a LED lighting project at the Library through a
  11| $40,000 state earmark received in FY22. Due to inflated construction costs that came about during the
  12| pandemic, the Flat Hill Road Culvert project estimated cost increased and the Town applied for a $500,000
  13| Small Bridge Grant to supplement the local appropriation.
  14|    Related to procurement of services, the Town entered into a contract with a new trash & recycling hauler,
  15|     E.L. Harvey, on July 1, 2021. The Town was contracted with Casella for the previous ten years and the
  16|     first few months of the new contract with E.L Harvey required significant attention to address prior
  17|     practices, such as pick-up on private roads that were not on the routes reviewed with Casella, to iron out.
  18|     Due to the increase in cost of the solid waste removal under the new contract, bag prices were required to
  19|     increase by 15% and this went into effect mid-August. In addition to the transition to a new vendor, the
  20|     pandemic’s effect on the supply of replacement trucks and driver shortages also impacted service
  21|     delivery.
  22|    The Town Manager’s Office applied for and received the MIIA Risk Management Grant for $10,000 for
  23|     Microsoft Office 365 licenses and $6,600 for a Recycling Dividends Grant that helped fund the Trash &
  24|     Recycling Coordinator position.
  25|    The Town Manager and staff worked closely with various industrial and commercial projects, including a
  26|     372,000 square foot warehouse/distribution facility called Lunenburg Central located on 475 Leominster-
  27|     Shirley Road and an adult use retail cannabis license applicant called Uma Flowers at 43 Summer Street.
  28|     The Select Board voted on the Host Community Agreement with Uma Flowers in December 2021, which
  29|     is a required step to get licensed by the Cannabis Control Commission.
  30|     Throughout the year the Select Board addressed various town property related items, including
  31|    The Select Board authorized Town Counsel to send two letters of Notice of Trespass to property owners
  32|     who were encroaching on town properties; the first was for boulders placed along the edge of and into the
  33|     public right of way at the rear of 993 Massachusetts Avenue and the other was for a shed at 38 Pierce
  34|     Avenue erected on town property. As a result of no action from the property owner at 993 Massachusetts
  35|     Avenue and on advice of Town counsel, the Select Board approved taking legal action to address the
  36|     encroachment of boulders within the public right of way.
  37|    The Select Board approved a survey requested by the Conservation Commission regarding the boundary
  38|     between    the   Cowdrey     Conservation      property   and   192   Townsend      Harbor    Road   to  rectify   a  possible
  39|     encroachment and the identification of other possible encroachments onto the                      Cowdrey property from
  40|     another property on Townsend Harbor Road and Woodland Drive. These possible encroachments were
  41|     discovered while the commission was performing site walks.
  42|    Authorized changes were made to some Assessors’                    records of town properties that were incorrectly
  43|     described as under the care of the Conservation Commission. The parcels were 510 Townsend Harbor
  44|     Road (split into two separate parcels)
  45|                                                                  20

   3|     amount of $3.5M. The Town Manager held meetings with department heads to solicit input on
   4|     potential ARPA projects and presented these to the Select Board. The Town Manager worked with a
   5|     company called Capital Strategic Solutions and they presented an outline of American Rescue Plan Act
   6|     funding categories and their services to the Board in February. The Board would continue to have
   7|     many discussions about how to spend the funding. These discussions are still occurring in FY 2023.

  11|     2023. The Town went out to bid for LED design and consultant services in March of 2022 but
  12|     ultimately decided to reject all bids. The Town Manager prepared and submitted the Green
  13|     Communities Competitive application for the conversion of the streetlights, administrative expenses,
  14|     and funds towards the purchase of a hybrid police cruiser with a grand total request of $187,140 The
  15|     Town was awarded this grant in August of 2022.

  17|     recommendation was to continue to include the 10-year staffing plan for the Police Department and the
  18|     5-year Fire/EMS plan to get to a full time Paramedic Level Service. Other priorities included additional
  19|     IT assistance and separating Facilities from the Department of Public Works to create a managerial
  20|     position that can manage all the capital projects and long-term planning for facilities. Another area she
  21|     recommended resources be allocated to is to address IT infrastructure, training, and preventative
  22|     measures that will protect the Town.
  23|    In October the Select Board met with the TC Passios Building Design Committee to review their three
  24|     draft warrant articles for the November Special Town Meeting, including an article to hear an interim
  25|     report on the committee’s progress, an article to authorize borrowing $28.9M for renovation of the
  26|     building for town offices, school uses, and community uses, and an article to fund the design and
  27|     engineering required to construct a new building for the consolidation of town offices, school uses, and
  28|     community space at the location of the building and land known as the TC Passios, amount to be
  29|     determined by Town Meeting. Ultimately the Committee presented an interim report but both funding
  30|     articles were passed over at the Special Town Meeting. After the Town Meeting, the Committee came
  31|     back to the Select Board and asked for a revised name to the Lunenburg Municipal Building Design
  32|     Committee and revised charge to include multiple buildings rather than just the TC Passios Building.
  33|    Prior to the November Special Town Meeting Select Board member Jim Marino submitted his resignation
  34|     due to his moving out of town. The Town Manager and the Board thanked him for all his years of service
  35|     and wished him well. The Board decided not to hold a special election to fill the vacancy.
  36|    The Special Town Meeting in November 2021 included a number of articles such as amending the
  37|     FY22 budget, payment of prior year bills, funding for the first year of the collective bargaining
  38|     agreement with the Firefighters Union, amending the Sewer Enterprise budget, transfer available funds
  39|     to the Sewer Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund, the three TCP Building Design warrant articles
  40|     mentioned above, acquiring temporary and permanent utility easements for construction of the culvert
  41|     on Flat Hill Road, approval for Select Board to acquire an easement related to the Lunenburg Central
  42|     parcel on Leominster Shirley Road, amendment to the sewer service area map by adding Marshall Park,
  43|     transferring 970 Rear New West Townsend Road to the care and custody of the Conservation
  44|     Commission, amending the Salary Administration Plan Bylaw to add certain positions, and an article
  45|     from the Historical Commission to amend to the Code of Lunenburg, Chapter 134, entitled Demolition
  46|     Delay, subsections 4-D and 5, and 4 articles to amend the Protective Bylaws.
  47|                                                                   21

===PAGE 22=== (layout, 51 lines)
   1|    In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday
   2|     entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.  Throughout the year, the
   3|     Board reviews Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers, flammable storage licenses, pole
   4|     petitions, tollbooth requests, First Right of Refusal Requests for Chapter 61 properties, and Dangerous
   5|     Dog hearings.
   6|    In January the Town Manager presented a Five-Year Forecast and Capital Plan for the funding of 20
   7|     projects totaling $1,740,049 and a 10 year look ahead at projected capital needs. In early March the
   8|     Town Manager revised the recommendation to include 3 more projects for a grand total of $2,162,849.
   9|    In February, the Select Board met with State Representative Kushmerek, State Senator John
  10|     Cronin, and State Representative Sena regarding FY 2023 budget priorities and any updates on
  11|     State budget or House/Senate Bills. The Town Manager presented her recommendations to the
  12|     Board on projects to submit as budget priorities and the Board voted a final list to submit to the
  13|     delegation that included the following projects:  $330,000 for Flat Hill Road culvert; $371,000 for
  14|     hazardous materials abatement and demolition of the old Primary School building; $100,000 for
  15|     Senior Center building improvements; $232,800 for the Air Conditioning for the Primary School.
  16|     The legislature included the $100,000 for the Senior Center building improvements for an ADA
  17|     outdoor patio and the Town received $100,000 in a bond bill towards the Primary School
  18|     building.
  19|    The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2023 to the Finance Committee and
  20|     Selectmen in early February.  Part of the Town Manager’s recommendation was to continue to fund the
  21|     Other Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the
  22|     amount of $266,696, an additional $50,000 towards Pavement Management, fund the regular
  23|     Stabilization fund in the amount of $100,000, and $450,000 towards the Special Purpose Stabilization
  24|     Fund. The FY2023 budget also included the hiring of an additional Full Time Patrolman, an additional
  25|     Full Time Firefighter, a new Facilities Director position, 2 part time Library Assistants for the Library to
  26|     open on Fridays, a new part time Assistant Meal Site Manager position, a part time Police
  27|     Administrative Assistant position, a part time IT Administrative Assistant position, a part time
  28|     Conservation Administrative Assistant, a $40K decrease for Solid Waste/Recycling Program, and a
  29|     3.11% increase for the Lunenburg Public Schools and Monty Tech. The total omnibus budget for FY23
  30|     that was approved in May was $41,568,744.
  31|    In May, following the annual election, the Board reorganized and vote Michael-Ray Jeffreys as the
  32|     new Chair, Tom Alonzo as Vice Chair and Lou Franco as Clerk. Heather Sroka and Todd Dwyer were
  33|     elected as the additional two members.
  34|    Powers and Sullivan presented the results of the FY 2021 Annual Audit and presented that the Town
  35|     had addressed any prior year comments.
  36|    The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY 2023.
  37|    The Town Manager’s Office submitted three Certificate to Alter applications for town buildings
  38|     within the Architectural Preservation District in FY 2022: the Town Hall for a building envelope
  39|     project, the Ritter Building for an ADA Ramp and replacement of windows, and to demolish the
  40|     vacant building known as the old Primary School on 30 School Street. The Select Board expressed
  41|     frustration at the inaction of the Architectural Preservation District Commission’s failure to act in a
  42|     timely manner regarding the Certificate to Alter application for the Ritter Building project submitted
  43|     in December 2021 as well as the APDC’s comments on social media regarding the 30 School Street
  44|     demolition project. Ultimately the APDC approved the Certificate to Alter application to demolish the
  45|     old Primary School on March 21, 2022, denied the Ritter Building Certificate to Alter application on
  46|     June 27, 2022 and approved the Town Hall Certificate to Alter application on July 6, 2022. The Town
  47|     is still pursuing an alteration to the Ritter Building including replacement of all the windows with
  48|     historic looking windows to become more energy efficient and install an ADA compliant ramp to the
  49|     entrance of the building, but this has been delayed due to the retirement of the Town’s original
  50|     architect. This continues to be a priority in 2023.

   1| 23
   2| Six and a half years has passed since I was appointed your Town Manager and I am thankful for the many
   3| residents I have met, work with on various committees, and represent on a daily basis overseeing the
   4| operations of the Town. Similar to last year, the pandemic and neighborly response that is demonstrated by the
   5| residents of Lunenburg, is inspiring. Thank you to all of our community members who continue to help their
   6| fellow residents throughout this pandemic.
   7| I have the privilege of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents and
   8| business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public.  The
   9| accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without the
  10| cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government turn.
  11| Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson and Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for all
  12| your hard work and keeping the office running smoothly.  I would also like to thank the committee and board
  13| members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  14| Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the current and former department heads that led our various
  15| departments in Fiscal Year 2022:  Thomas Gammel, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Robert Oliva,
  16| former DPW Director; Bill Bernard, current DPW Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance
  17| Director; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Sue Byrne, former Principal Assessor; CJ Carroll, Principal
  18| Assessor; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA Director; Muir Haman, Library Director;
  19| Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk;  and all their staff for their talent and working together to make our town run as
  20| efficiently as possible.  Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses that make Lunenburg a wonderful
  21| place to visit, work, and live.
  22| Respectfully submitted,
  23| Heather R. Lemieux

  25| Town Manager

===PAGE 39=== (layout, 25 lines)
   1|                                                  BOARD OF ASSESSORS
   2| The Board of Assessors is responsible for establishing the value of property, both real and personal, for
   3| taxation purposes for the town. Assessed values are subject to a five-year revaluation and interim year
   4| adjustments for certification by the Department of Revenue.  The Assessors grant all statutory exemptions for
   5| Senior and Surviving Spouse, Blind and Veterans. They act on all abatement applications to property values.
   6| The Assessors are responsible for defending all appeals to the Appellate Tax Board. They also administer all
   7| special assessments qualifying farm and forest management programs. Assessments in Massachusetts are
   8| based on full and fair cash value as of January 1st.
   9| The Assessors analyze all sales from 2021 for FY 23 valuations.  Market value has seen increases in the sales
  10| of homes in town.  Properties have been selling over the assessed valuations reflecting an overall increase to
  11| market value of the town. The next town wide recertification will occur this year for FY 2024.
  12| Each property in town has a property record card on file with all the components that go into the valuation of
  13| the parcel - such as land size, building description, sales information, etc.  We recommend that the taxpayers
  14| review their property field card annually to ensure all the information is up to date and correct. The property
  15| record cards are available in our office or online through the GIS maps.
  16| The Department of Revenue certified the results of assessment data to be within their statistical requirements
  17| and the tax bills went out on time.  The tax rate went from $17.19 to $14.62 for FY 2023.
  18| The office continues the process of mandated cyclical inspections of all properties throughout the town.  This
  19| is achieved by inspections, visiting the properties, and by means of sales verification, building permits,
  20| occupancy permits and abatement inspections.  The Board of Assessors strives to maintain accuracy in the
  21| assessment to sales ratio as well as assessment uniformity.
  22| The office staff strives to continue to provide good service that the Lunenburg taxpayers have come to expect.
  23| Respectfully submitted,
  24| Richard Letarte, Chairman      Christine C.Higdon, Member      Kevin Fish, Member
  25|                                                                     39

   2| BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   3| The Building Department is located in the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960 Massachusetts Avenue.
   4| The department consists of Casey Burlingame who is the Building Commissioner and Zoning Enforcement
   5| Officer. Lisa Normandin is the Administrative Assistant to the Building Commissioner. Jack Biery is the
   6| Electrical Inspector, and Gary Williams is the Plumbing and Gas Inspector.
   7| The Building Department strives to ensure all work done in the Town of Lunenburg is performed to state
   8| mandated regulations and codes, as well as local zoning bylaws. In addition to Building, Plumbing, Gas,
   9| and Electrical permits, the Building Department also oversees annual inspections for places of assembly,
  10| safety inspections for licensed on-premise alcohol establishments and Forms of Intent required for business
  11| certificates issued from the Town Clerk office.
  12| In fiscal year 2022 the following permits were issued:
  13| 620 Building Permits
  14| 282 Electrical Permits
  15| 107 Plumbing Permits
  16| 127 Gas Permits
  17| 1,136 total permits issued
  18| The state of Massachusetts is currently utilizing the 2015 International Building Code and 2015
  19| International Residential Code, as amended by Massachusetts. The Town continues to utilize ViewPoint
  20| online permitting software and all applications for work requiring a permit can be done online. In addition,
  21| paper applications are available at the Building Department office.
  22| CEMETERY COMMISSION
  23| The Cemetery Commission and Superintendent Todd Holman met regularly throughout the year to discuss
  24| cemetery business.  Projects that were worked on included surveying the Goodrich family burial yard on the
  25| corner of Fish and Lincoln Streets.  The survey established the property boundaries in preparation of
  26| extending the existing stone wall to enclose the burial plots.  The final resting places of seven additional
  27| family members were located using ground penetrating radar.  An appropriate grave marker will be placed
  28| identifying all the interred in due time.  A ground penetrating radar survey was performed at the Arnold/
  29| Litch family burial yard on West Street Terrace, and the seven burial plots were located.
  30| The project to prune and restore the historic hedges that line Holman Street at the North Cemetery began
  31| this year with the expectation it will be fully completed by the end of next year.  Monuments honoring the
  32| Revolutionary War soldiers that are buried at the North and South Cemeteries were erected.
  33| The Commissioners would like to thank the following people and businesses whose help was invaluable
  34| this year: DPW Director William Bernard for his guidance and support during the year.  Superintendent
  35| Todd Holman for his continued dedication to the care of the town cemeteries and to the service he provides
  36| for families in their time of need. Seasonal Laborers Alexis Dimond and Danielle Peterson for the
  37| dedication and hard work they put in this year in helping the Superintendent maintain the cemeteries.
  38| Robert Ullo for his assistance in plowing snow away from the entrance to South Cemetery. Dave Passios
  39| for his volunteer efforts in keeping the well-pumps in good working order. Leominster Monument Co. for
  40| their continued assistance whenever it is needed. Mrs. Helen Balcom for her services in planting,
  41| maintaining, and watering the flowers that decorate the entrances to both cemeteries.
  42| The Cemetery Commission maintains an online mapping system for both cemeteries that can be found at:
  43| www.burialsearch.com
  44| William Tyler, Chairman
  45| Nancy- Foss Yasko, Vice- Chairman
  46| David Dorren, Clerk

   1| 56
   2| CONSERV ATION COMMISSION
   3| The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven member commission, appointed to  three year terms by
   4| the Select Board, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL c. 40, § 8C. The
   5| Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL c. 131, § 40 and Chapter 239 of the local bylaws
   6| for the protection of wetlands.  The Commission also manages and controls town-owned lands retained for conservation
   7| and passive recreation use.
   8| The year 2022 was as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and meetings for a
   9| wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep projects and larger
  10| scale industrial/commercial proposals.
  11| The Commission held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to meetings to
  12| familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The Commission continues to
  13| work with the Town on familiarizing itself with the continually changing storm water mandates from the EPA and
  14| working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification as well as aiding with the
  15| community implementations the “MS4” regulations.
  16| The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans developed using grants as outlined in
  17| the last two annual reports. The upcoming management efforts will be concentrated on the Rayno property on
  18| Northfield Road and the Hawes property on Hollis Road. The stewardship plan for the Hollis Road property is oriented
  19| toward enhancing bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the DCR "Forestry for
  20| the Birds" program.  The forest stewardship plans which provide environmental management including timber-stand
  21| management were developed by Gary Gouldrup of New England Forestry Consultants. The Commission continues to
  22| hold public forums on the implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management. The funding from
  23| the sale of harvested timber provides the seed money to make it possible for the Commission to apply for grants to
  24| maintain and improve Lunenburg’s conservation lands.
  25| The Commission continues to implement a Recreational Trails Program grant providing color-coded trail markers and
  26| coordinated “You Are Here” trail map signs to expand the safe use of Lunenburg’s existing trail network.  Regional
  27| trail system map signs show how trails are interconnected among multiple properties. The project also provides
  28| informational kiosks and emergency access gates.  During 2022 trail maps were developed for and continue to be
  29| refined in detail for eleven conservation properties.  These trail maps are posted on the new Conservation Commission
  30| Trails website, on informational kiosks on the properties, and on “You Are Here” trail map signs at trail heads and trail
  31| intersections. The Commission would like to express our gratitude for all the hard-working volunteers that work so
  32| enthusiastically in maintaining and improving Lunenburg’s conservation lands. The Lunenburg Snow Riders continue
  33| to support the maintenance and improvement of the trail system on Lunenburg’s conservation lands.  The club replaced
  34| a bridge on the Cowdrey Conservation Area.  The club was awarded a Recreational Trails Program grant that will erect
  35| five new trail head kiosks, improve the cart paths on the Lane Conservation Area, level and de-stump Small Town
  36| Forest trails and gravel the parking lots at the Cowdrey Conservation Area.  The Conservation Commission will fund
  37| the material for this work using Timber Rights Funds.
  38| The Commission has continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills on the implementations of their
  39| lake management plans.  These plans set goals and action items designed to give a better picture of the overall health of
  40| the lakes and define various strategies to ensure continued protection of these valuable resources.
  41| During the year of 2022, change of administration occurred along with an additional staff member. In the month of May
  42| Conservation Administrator Matt Marro retired after many years of service to the Conservation Commission and Town
  43| of Lunenburg. After his departure, Nadia Madden had taken his place as Conservation Administrator. The town
  44| additionally hired a Conservation Administrative Assistant, Rebecca Manley, who has been a great help for the
  45| Conservation Administrator. In November 2022, Anna Petrie was hired as Conservation Administrator after Nadia’s
  46| departure.
  47| The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!
  48| Respectfully Submitted,
  49| Bob Pease, Chair                                      Mary Wilson, Vice-Chair                  Richard Tremblay, Clerk
  50| Richard Bursch                                         Catie Childs                                       Michael Larouche
  51|                                                                   Jeffrey Viviano
  52|                                               Anna Petrie, Conservation Administrator

===PAGE 57=== (layout, 46 lines)
   1|                                                          COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|                                                                       FY22
   3|                                                       July 1, 2021 – June 30, 2022
   4| The mission of the Council on Aging                                                                   is to advocate for the improved
   5| quality of life for all seniors through                                                               supportive services, programs, and
   6| education.  Councils on Aging were                                                                    established under Massachusetts
   7| General Law Chapter 40, section 8B,                                                                   to include setting policy for the
   8| administration of elder programs or                                                                   services and to develop, coordinate,
   9| and conduct such activities which                                                                     advocate for elder issues.
  10| The FY22 Council on Aging staff                                                                       consisted of Susan Doherty,
  11| Director; Amanda Koeck,                                                                               Administrative Assistant and
  12| Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach                                                                 Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal
  13| Site Coordinator; Elsa Watson and Ann Penney, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, David Gallagher, Peter
  14| Tomlinson, and Kimberly Moore, MART Van Drivers.
  15| The Council on Aging Board is an eleven-member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Select Board. The
  16| FY22 Council on Aging Board members were Chairperson Deb Lincoln,  Vice-Chairperson Jane Rabbitt, Barbara
  17| Brown, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Pete Lincoln, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, John Pesenti, Susan O                              ’Sullivan, and
  18| Judith Tarbell.  Jacquelyn Dwyer joined the board in May of 2022 to fill the remaining term of Sarah Grant.
  19| The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House Supporters
  20| Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and Montachusett
  21| Regional Transit Authority.
  22| We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Council on Aging.  In FY22 we had 40 volunteers who
  23| contributed 1183.25 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on Wheels program,
  24| program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, Discovery Center navigators, and friendly visitors/
  25| phone calls.  We greatly appreciate their service.
  26| In FY22 we offered a variety of programs including Meals on Wheels, congregate lunch and breakfast programs,
  27| transportation, health and wellness, arts and crafts, intergenerational, bocce, shuffleboard, cards and games, cultural,
  28| information and referral, Memory Café, health insurance counseling, and social events both at the senior center and
  29| offsite to people age 50+.  There were approximately 1337 people who utilized the senior center in FY22.  Those 1337
  30| people compiled 19,162 units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
  31| The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
  32| www.mycommunityonline.com , or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town Hall,
  33| Library, Post Office, local churches, and various businesses throughout the community.
  34| We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
  35| supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
  36| Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc.  This group provides funding for new
  37| living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, and garden upkeep at the Eagle House
  38| Senior Community Center.  Also special thanks to all our outside agencies, especially the Lunenburg Police and Fire
  39| Departments, for their time and funding for support of programming for our clients.
  40| I want to thank the staff and volunteers at the Eagle House Senior Community Center for their caring and commitment to
  41| our Lunenburg elders.  There are 350 Councils on Aging in the Commonwealth, and I consider our Lunenburg staff and
  42| volunteers amongst the “very best of the best” in Massachusetts.
  43| Respectfully Submitted
  44| Susan Doherty, Director
  45| Lunenburg Council on Aging
  46|                                                                         57

   1|                                                      CULTURAL COUNCIL
   2| The Lunenburg Cultural Council with grants from Massachusetts Cultural Council has fully funded or
   3| contributed to twenty-three art, music, cultural and science programs to benefit the residents of Lunenburg and
   4| surrounding communities.
   5| These include events such as children         ’s programs at the Public Library, adult programs at the Eagle House, the
   6| 85th Regional Art Show at Fitchburg Art Museum, an art therapy program for cancer patients and survivors,
   7| tuition for one student for the 2022 Cannon Theater Children’s Program, and the Discovery Museum, among
   8| other programs.
   9| FY22 grants totaled $10,640. Because of the pandemic, some FY21 programs were moved into FY22 and
  10| those were also funded ($1180), with only a few cancelled due to the pandemic.
  11| We are looking forward to the events in FY23. Grants were approved for twenty-two programs totaling
  12| $14,166.
  13| In addition, the Cultural Council will be sponsoring several programs of our own, including two day-long art
  14| classes which will be held at the Public Library this spring.
  15| We are looking forward to holding more events during the year and hope all will enjoy and benefit from the
  16| cultural events in Lunenburg.

===PAGE 59=== (layout, 48 lines)
   1|                                                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2| The Department of Public Works is the resources of the Highway, Cemetery, Sewer, Vehicle Maintenance, Facilities,
   3| and Parks divisions combined to maintain roads, bridges, sewer infrastructure, Town buildings, and parks and to provide
   4| services essential to the welfare and acceptable quality of life for the citizens of Lunenburg.
   5| Fiscal Year 2022 was transitional year for the DPW with the Director, Robert Oliva, two Heavy Equipment Operators,
   6| and the Recreation Director leaving for other employment opportunities and the hiring of a new DPW Director, William
   7| Bernard.  Major changes continued in early Fiscal Year 2023 with the establishment of a new Facilities Department and
   8| the removal of the Facilities and Parks Divisions from under the umbrella of the DPW.  Despite the upheaval, the DPW
   9| accomplished much work while providing excellent services.  One mainstay throughout all was the DPW Executive
  10| Assistant, Samantha Lenox, who provided business and professional support to all staff.
  11| Departmental Mission:
  12| The mission of the Department of Public Works is to provide high quality, cost effective, efficient, and safe services to
  13| protect, preserve and improve the Town’s roadway infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and
  14| enhances the current and future social and economic needs of the community; to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality
  15| environment for the Town’s citizens and visitors; and to provide environmentally sound means of solid waste and
  16| wastewater disposal.
  17| Departmental Goals and Objectives
  18|     Be prepared to respond to any weather, roadway infrastructure, or accident-related emergency that may arise on a
  19|      twenty-four hour per day, three hundred and sixty-five day per year basis.
  20|     Maintain and enhance a highly qualified and dedicated staff of public works professionals.
  21|     Provide preventative maintenance in a proactive manner to reduce reactive maintenance as follows:
  22|     Maintain and update the sewer system and associated pumping stations.
  23|     Maintain and repair roadways in accordance with the new (2022) pavement management plan.
  24|     Maintain and repair sidewalks in accordance with the 2020 PROW ADA Transition plan.
  25|     Increase the number of roadsides mowed by developing a roadside maintenance plan.
  26|     Increase the number of catch basin and stormwater structures cleaned and the number of miles of roadways swept
  27|      per the June 2020 Operations and Maintenance Plan prepared by Comprehensive Environmental, Inc.
  28|     Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and plans for
  29|      rehabilitation.
  30|     Increase training offered by our insurance provider to obtain credits to lessen premiums.
  31|     Identify new sources (Federal, State, and other grants) of funding to supplement the operational budget.
  32|     Continue to maintain a high level of courteous communication and productive interaction between members of
  33|      boards and commissions as well as Town residents.
  34|     Strive to modernize the operations of the Department by obtaining and using the most up to date equipment and
  35|      methods, including asset management.
  36| Highway Division
  37| The highway division provided daily maintenance and repairs of roads, drainage, signs, trees, roadsides, sewers, and
  38| more.  They assisted all other Departments as requested and responded to emergencies 24 hours per day as needed.  The
  39| Highway Superintendent, Joseph Progin, provided outstanding leadership, knowledge of the town’s infrastructure and
  40| demonstrated true concern for the citizens of Lunenburg.  The DPW Director reviewed and approved applications for
  41| permits for Road Openings, Street Improvements, Trench Safety, Sewer Connections, and Sewer Extensions. He
  42| reviewed applications for driveway installations, site plans, stormwater BMP's, building demolition, and sign
  43| installations for DPW related issues.
  44| Bridge L-17-013 (6T2) - Pleasant Street Over Pearl Hill Brook
  45| The replacement of the bridge was completed in the fall of 2021.  Funding for the reconstruction of the Pleasant Street
  46| Bridge was obtained through the MassDOT Small Bridge Program.  The bridge was designed by our engineering
  47| consultant, VHB, and R. Bates & Son was the construction contractor with construction beginning in May 2021.
  48|                                                                         59

===PAGE 60=== (layout, 49 lines)
   1|                                                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2| Pavement Management
   3| The Town first developed a Pavement Management Plan in 2009.  This plan was updated in 2015 and defined a yearly
   4| paving plan through Fiscal Year 2023 and included a $4,000,000 voter authorized debt exclusion.  The goal of the plan
   5| was to achieve an overall town-wide Road Surface Rating of 72-75 out of 100.  In July of 2022 the DPW contracted with
   6| BETA Group, Inc. to have them provide a new pavement management plan as the current plan was ending and the debt
   7| exclusion funding had been exhausted.  Although the work was completed in Fiscal Year 2023 and not Fiscal Year 2022,
   8| it is worth revealing that BETA Group surveyed our roads using LIDAR and reported an overall Road Surface Rating of
   9| 84.75.  The Project Manager for BETA Group commented “it is one of the highest scores I’ve seen in New England over
  10| the past 12 years that I’ve been doing this”.  The DPW is proud to have successfully administered the plan and
  11| anticipates carrying out the newly developed plan in a similar manner.
  12| The following roads were paved with hot mix asphalt this year:
  13| Brown Ave.                      Goodrich Street                             Highland Street                              Main Street
  14| Memorial Drive                Oak Avenue                                  Pleasant Street
  15| Storm Water
  16| Staying in compliance with our EPA NPDES MS4 Permit continues to remain an important function of the DPW.  Along
  17| with the Storm Water Task Force, Land Use Director, and our Storm Water Consultant, the DPW works to meet the
  18| obligations of the permit and required reporting each year.  The DPW provides maintenance and care of the Town                              ’s
  19| storm water management systems.  This includes, but is not limited to catch basin cleaning, street sweeping, pipe jetting,
  20| replacing pipes, and swale cleaning.  The Department responds to calls for clogged catch basins and culverts to help
  21| control ponding and flooding.
  22| Tree Removal
  23| A Tree Risk Management Inventory of almost 1,200 trees on the roads in Lunenburg was completed in 2015.  The
  24| inventory prioritizes the removal and/or pruning of the trees.  However, storm damage and natural attrition creates a
  25| backlog of tree work that exceeds the Department’s allotted budget for tree removal.  The Department strives to fulfill
  26| each resident’s request for tree work but must prioritize work based upon the determined degree of danger to person and
  27| property and location.  The DPW will continue to act in a reactionary manner as needs arise.  All tree work other than
  28| brush removal, chipping, and the removal of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the lack of a
  29| bucket truck or crane and a licensed tree professional.  Each spring and after storms, the DPW staff remove and dispose
  30| of woody debris within the Town’s right-of-way.  An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the
  31| burden of the Town by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.  The
  32| DPW appreciates and thanks Unitil for their responses to remove hazardous trees when asked to do so.
  33| Roadside Vegetation Maintenance
  34| The DPW staff responded to many requests to cut and clear overgrown vegetation from roadsides.  This work is done
  35| using lawn mowers, saws, and other hand tools as the existing roadside mower was disabled.   A new utility tractor with
  36| a boom rotary mower attachment (22-foot horizontal reach and 19.5-foot vertical reach) was ordered in September of
  37| 2022.   The DPW is developing a town-wide program of selective curbside mowing and vegetation clearing using the
  38| new tractor and boom mower that will significantly enhance our roadside vegetation maintenance program.
  39| Landfill
  40| The closed and capped landfill off Youngs Road is inspected by a consultant semiannually and remains in compliance
  41| with the requirements of the Massachusetts Division of Environmental Protection (DEP).  Gas monitoring and
  42| groundwater contamination sampling and testing is conducted by a consultant.  The landfill site including the perimeter
  43| fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  44| Adjacent to the closed landfill is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be
  45| hazardous waste by the EPA.  The DEP has issued a Beneficial Use Determination (BUD) permit which allows the
  46| storage of these materials.  A consultant performs the yearly testing of these materials.
  47| On seven Saturdays in the fall and the spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage Yard Waste Days.  Residents may
  48| drop off brush, leaves, and other organic materials for composting.
  49|                                                                        60

===PAGE 61=== (layout, 48 lines)
   1|                                                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2| Solid Waste
   3| Solid waste (trash) is collected curbside weekly under the Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program by our vendor E.L.
   4| Harvey & Sons, Inc.  Recyclable materials are also collected curbside weekly by Harvey.  Pay as You Throw bags may
   5| be purchased at Hannaford Supermarket, Smokestack Roasters, Maki Building Center, Honey Farms, and Jaxx Country
   6| Variety.  Residents may call E. L. Harvey to arrange the pickup of bulky items for a fee.  The DPW remains the point of
   7| contact between residents and E. L. Harvey.  Residents may dispose of household hazardous wastes for a fee at the
   8| Devens Regional Household Hazardous Waste Collection Center on the published dates and times.
   9| Lake Shirley Dam
  10| In the early 1990’s, with the structure in need of repair, The Lake Shirley Management Corporation Association turned
  11| over ownership of the dam to the Town.  After the completion of the total reconstruction of the dam, the responsibility
  12| for the maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping were completed by a small number of volunteers from the Lake
  13| Association.  Compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well as an
  14| emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of the Department of Public Works in 2004-2005.   The dam is
  15| inspected by a professional engineer licensed in Massachusetts.  The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report,
  16| and we remain in compliance with regulations.
  17| Winter Operations
  18| The DPW was prepared for and responded to all snow and ice weather events with the goal of providing a high level of
  19| service in a timely manner while at the same time trying to minimize costs.  Lunenburg joins with 17 other towns in a
  20| cooperative bid to obtain the best possible pricing for rock salt.  However, the price of rock salt continues to steadily
  21| increase every year.  The DPW maintains a small fleet of snow removal equipment (trucks, plows, spreaders, loader,
  22| backhoe, and sidewalk plow).  The fleet is supplemented with vendors as needed.  Due to breakdowns of the aged
  23| Town’s fleet of large dump trucks, more vendors were needed; thus, adding to costs.
  24| The clearing of snow and ice from roadways is a critical operation of the DPW as it is a matter of public safety.
  25| The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private vendors who tirelessly
  26| and successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regardless of the severity or duration of a storm.                      “DPW
  27| workers are the original Storm Troopers.”
  28| Cemetery
  29| The maintenance of the Town’s cemeteries was performed by the dedicated Cemetery Division Superintendent, Todd
  30| Holman, and two seasonal cemetery laborers.  The Superintendent coordinates burials with families and funeral homes
  31| and meets regularly with the three-member Cemetery Commission.  The Highway Division provides equipment and staff
  32| for maintenance and burials, which on average is forty per year.
  33| Wastewater
  34| The DPW is responsible for the day-to-day operation and maintenance of the wastewater collection system.  A specialty
  35| vendor is contracted to maintain, repair, and respond to emergencies at the 10 sewer pump stations.  The outstanding
  36| Sewer Business Manager, Barbara Lefebvre, manages the administrative functions of the Sewer Division including
  37| billing, customer service, permitting and budgets.  An Assistant Sewer Business Manager, Paula Bertram, was hired to
  38| help facilitate the many tasks of the division.  The Sewer Business Manager and DPW Director worked closely with the
  39| Sewer Commission to ensure sewer rules, regulations, and policies are followed and to assure the future long-term
  40| operation of the wastewater collection system.
  41| Fleet Maintenance
  42| The skilled and dedicated DPW Mechanic, James Sticklor, is responsible for the preventative maintenance and repair of
  43| all Town vehicles.  The vehicles include over 30 pieces of highway construction equipment, high performance police
  44| vehicles, Fire and Rescue first responder apparatus, mowers and more.  Highly technical repairs and repairs that cannot
  45| be completed in-house are outsourced to specialty vendors as needed.  A fleet maintenance software program continues
  46| to be a valuable tool as each repair is logged by vehicle identification number and stored in a comprehensive database
  47| and required services, repair costs over the life of the equipment, and part numbers are easily obtained.
  48|                                                                         61

===PAGE 63=== (layout, 50 lines)
   1|                                                 FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|                                                                  Fiscal Year 2023
   3| Annual Town Meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on warrant articles that
   4| directly impact how we manage our town. Amongst these articles is the FY23 budget, capital plan, authorizations for
   5| various enterprise and revolving funds, and appropriations relating to stabilization funds and reserves. Throughout the
   6| year the Town Manager and department heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful conversations about how
   7| to best serve and manage the town. While developing the budget they considered funding, contracts, and mandates, as
   8| well as what the community wants and needs to thrive. The Town Manager formulated a recommended budget, which
   9| she presented to the Finance Committee on February 17th. Over six subsequent Finance Committee meetings,
  10| department heads presented overviews and plans relating to their budgets and capital requests. A Public Hearing was
  11| held on March 31st, as required by the Town Charter, and the Finance Committee voted on recommendations pertaining
  12| to financially relevant articles.
  13| Armed with all the information gathered during this process, the Finance Committee recommends approval of the Town
  14| Manager’s omnibus budget, as outlined in this warrant.
  15| Overall, our financial condition is good. Revenues are stable, aided by strong collections, increases in state aid, new
  16| growth, and a variety of federal and state grants that the town has secured. On the expense side, while not meeting every
  17| need outlined by the department heads, this budget does begin to address several emerging and previously unmet needs.
  18| The total in requested expenditures is $49,953,531.76. While we are pleased with our current fiscal standing, we do face
  19| challenges ahead; especially with regard to a number of our municipal buildings, which we outline in more detail below.
  20| Notably, our certified free cash this year is $2,666,962, which is substantially higher than in previous years, a temporary
  21| benefit resulting from appropriations that were not fully expended in FY21 as a result of COVID                        -related restrictions and
  22| impacts. Town fiscal policy directs portions of free cash to accounts such as stabilization, the OPEB allocation (a fund to
  23| pay for future retiree benefits), and the capital plan.
  24| Accordingly, this year’s recommended capital plan (funded by free cash, tax revenues, and unspent capital
  25| appropriations from previous years) is $2,162,849. It is an unprecedented investment of available funds (without
  26| incurring any additional debt) into the capital infrastructure that supports our town. As of the printing of this warrant, th                      e
  27| Finance Committee is seeking to answer a few specific questions pertaining to the capital plan. We will have a
  28| recommendation on the capital plan, Article 6, at Town Meeting on May 7.
  29| Other notable items:
  30|     For FY23, the proposed appropriation for education is $23,397,371, 56.2%% of the Town’s total budget, and a
  31|      3.78% increase over last year. This includes our Monty Tech assessment at $1,054,376, which is a 20.4% increase
  32|      for FY23, mainly due to an increase in enrollment from 83 to 94 students. Additionally, the town budget includes
  33|      $1,207,421 in School Retiree Health Insurance. Fortunately, this year the town benefited from an $898,610 increase
  34|      in Chapter 70 aid, which more than offsets the $673,617 increase in the Lunenburg School District budget.
  35| While our Chapter 70 increase was welcome news, the increase was partly a result of formula changes that recognized
  36| more low-income students at a higher tier of funding. Our vulnerable populations are of top concern for all of our
  37| department leaders.
  38| An area of concern for the Finance Committee are several personnel positions that the School District is funding through
  39| ESSER 2 and ESSER 3 federal grants. These grants, totaling $1,939,868 will expire in FY24. Many of these positions
  40| are addressing acute health, academic, and social/emotional needs that have arisen from COVID-19. Over the next two
  41| years the School Committee will need to determine what positions we need to maintain, and how to fund them.,
  42|     This year’s budget supports an increase of $53,443 for the library budget to support the addition of two part-time
  43|      personnel and additional expenses. This increase will enable the library to extend its operating hours to include
  44|      opening on Fridays.
  45| ●     This year’s budget continues to support long-term staffing and investment programs that we committed to in
  46| previous years, including an additional full-time firefighter and police officer. We are again increasing road pavement
  47| management funding by $50,000. We are also contributing $450,000 to the special-purpose stabilization fund, which
  48| should give us the option to pay cash for ambulance and fire truck replacements in coming years, as opposed to
  49| borrowing or using free cash.
  50|                                                                          63

===PAGE 64=== (layout, 49 lines)
   1|     This year’s budget includes a 4.71% increase in IT spending, for a total of $316,022.89. In addition, the Lunenburg
   2|      School District has budgeted $330,514 for technology-related administration and educational expenses. In late
   3|      FY21, the town began the process of transferring the function of IT strategy and leadership to a private firm, which
   4|      works in close consultation with town and school department leadership. Following a comprehensive review, an
   5|      upgrade of networking and cloud services has commenced, which will continue into FY23. Additionally, the town is
   6|      instituting rotational upgrades of computers and peripherals throughout our operations. The town website will also
   7|      get regular upgrades. These programs are drastically improving the quality and reliability of our IT infrastructure and
   8|      improving our security posture. The Finance Committee was pleased with the ‘out-of-the-box’ thinking and
   9|      interdepartmental cooperation. We have encouraged all departments to find similar synergies and opportunities for
  10|      collaboration and cost sharing.
  11|     As we have highlighted in previous years, Lunenburg’s debt ratio has been identified by an independent credit-rating
  12|      agency as the sole reason that we are unable to achieve the highest-available bond rating. The good news is that our
  13|      total debt and our debt service are declining. Our Excludable Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 9.18%, nearly 2% below
  14|      the 11% cap. Our Net Regular Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 1.22% in FY 23, well-below the 4% cap in our fiscal
  15|      policy. Our debt service will continue to decline over the next two years as bonds for the Public Safety Building,
  16|      Primary School, and Library come off the debt schedule. However, this bit of good news could be temporary. There
  17|      are several projects currently under discussion, which if acted upon and funded by borrowing, could exceed our debt
  18|      limits in coming years. The Municipal Building Design Committee will likely have recommendations in coming
  19|      months pertaining to Town Hall, Ritter, and the TC Passios building. The Select Board is considering re-
  20|      development of the old Primary School property at 30 School Street. The Parks Commission is formulating a master
  21|      plan for Marshall Park and is investigating issues with the Town Beach building. And the Lunenburg School District
  22|      anticipates a renovation or replacement of Turkey Hill Elementary School within the next ten years.
  23|     The warrant includes two articles that, if approved as presented, will make progress in addressing the old Primary
  24|      School property at 30 School Street. Article 6, the capital plan, appropriates $371,000 for hazardous materials
  25|      abatement. Article 19 appropriates $450,000 for the demolition of the building and engineering plans for the
  26|      property’s redevelopment into a municipal park.
  27|     During FY22, the town received $3,507,971 from the federal government resulting from The American Rescue Plan
  28|      Act (ARPA). These funds are not accounted for in the FY23 budget. Recent guidance from the U.S. Treasury gives
  29|      municipal executives (in our case the Select Board) fairly wide latitude for appropriating these funds. The Select
  30|      Board will soon be gathering opinions and ideas pertaining to how we should use these funds. Potentially, some of
  31|      this money could be used to offset costs relating to some of the projects mentioned above.
  32| Lunenburg’s overall good fiscal condition is the result of adherence to conservative fiscal policy recommended by the
  33| Town Manager, enacted by the Select Board, and enabled by Town Meeting appropriations. There is no doubt that we
  34| will face new challenges in the coming years. In addition to some of the concerns outlined above, inflation is a looming
  35| headwind that will likely drive-up labor, capital, and materials costs. We believe that the investments we are making
  36| today will give us greater flexibility and capacity to address future challenges.
  37| The Finance Committee thanks Town Manager Heather Lemieux and Finance Director, Karen Brochu for their tireless
  38| dedication and leadership throughout the year, and especially during budget season. We also thank our department heads
  39| and all of our municipal and school employees for their exemplary service to the citizens of Lunenburg and stewardship
  40| of our town.

===PAGE 66=== (layout, 45 lines)
   1|               "Whatever the cost of our libraries, the price is cheap compared to that of an ignorant
   2|               nation." -Walter Cronkite
   3| As the pandemic stretched into Fiscal Year 2022, we saw lingering disruption of old patterns and new patterns of
   4| behavior continuing. One constant, however, seems to repeat itself: the library builds value for the community.
   5| The library has continued to follow its strategic plan this year. Keeping our mission close to our hearts:
   6|           Lunenburg Public Library welcomes all and serves residents of the Lunenburg area as a hub for the community,
   7|           enriching our lives through its collections, programs, services, facility, and people.
   8| Our library resources reach far into the community. You no longer need to visit the library in person to access our
   9| resources. The library has focused on improving and expanding the number of resources available digitally, and made
  10| concerted efforts to ease access to those resources. We also added Niche Academy to help patrons learn about our digital
  11| resources and how to access them. Another new resource that will be much appreciated is Mango Languages, offering a
  12| convenient way to learn over 70 foreign languages, with a focus on conversations.
  13| Patrons can use all of our databases, e-books and e-audiobooks, our website (lunenburglibrary.org), and even streaming
  14| services like Kanopy from their homes or cars or workplace. You could even attend a virtual program or interact with
  15| library staff for help on a question or a reading recommendation without visiting the library. Of course, if you never
  16| visited, you would be missing out on the beautiful building.
  17| You would also be missing out on one of the most important services we offer: library programs. Although attendance in
  18| -person during FY22 was not as consistent as it was immediately before the pandemic, there are still programs
  19| happening at the library nearly every day, and our consistency paid dividends during the summer, as attendance at
  20| programs really returned to a normal level. During the year library staff focused on providing access to programs
  21| virtually and in-person, and continuing to build a dependable, consistent calendar of events that would appeal to a variety
  22| of people.
  23| Library staff have also focused on trying to find new ways for residents to engage with our library collection.
  24| Particularly worthy of note are curated experiences that have been very popular. Individuals even travel from nearby
  25| communities to try out these kits. Our Adult Services Librarian, Lydia Gravel, introduced the idea with “Mystery Travel
  26| Kits” which create an enriching learning experience mimicking some parts of travelling to a foreign country by using
  27| items in the library collection. Each kit typically contains several books, a movie or two, music, and some activities or
  28| games. These kits are still being curated and can be requested through our online event calendar on our website.
  29| As the year went on, we saw more and more people actually visiting the library. Each afternoon during the school year,
  30| we have been seeing more students than ever before. Partnering with the Boys and Girls Club brings dozens of students
  31| to study during the afternoon Power Hour. In addition to that, we often see dozens of other teens, spending time at the
  32| library. They play video games, study quietly, eat snacks, and sometimes make a little too much noise. We are, however,
  33| excited that they have chosen to come to the library after school.
  34| We continue to focus on stewarding the facility for the future. Efficiency and sustainability are priorities for the trustees
  35| and the director. This year we completed replacement of a number of lighting fixtures and bulbs, so that we are now
  36| almost completely lit by LEDs. The HVAC system has also been at the forefront of our attention, as it is beginning to
  37| show the signs of its age. Increased maintenance needs are typical of an aging system, and ours is no exception. We will
  38| continue to make responsible plans for the facility, so that it will be a gem for Lunenburg for decades to come.
  39| Adult Services Notes
  40|     We launched 4 reading challenges for adults, using Beanstack logging software to record:
  41|                          66 readers who joined in at least one challenge, reading more than 245,733 minutes
  42|                              683 badges were earned for specific goals or milestones within the challenges
  43|     We hosted 105 programs and meetings for our adult patrons and 1,040 people attended.
  44|                              13 of these events were held virtually, mostly on the Zoom meeting platform.
  45|                                                                            66

   1|    9 were take-home kits with craft supplies and instructions, or a curated selection of Library materials. The most
   2|     popular kit was new for FY22: the Mystery Travel Kits, which has been such a success it is continuing to be offered
   3|     on a regular basis
   4|     We partnered with several community organizations to bring other local perspectives to our programming initiatives,
   5|        including the Lunenburg Adult Activity Center at Eagle House, Worcester Poetry groups, the North County Land
