   7| Adult Service Goals for FY23 & Beyond
   8| 1.  Identify areas of programming that should be developed with greater intention, and experiment with new ideas to
   9| keep events fresh.
  10| 2. Cultivate deeper partnerships with local institutions and groups, including offsite programs, thereby becoming a more
  11| recognizable and connected resource in our community.
  12| 3. Encourage meaningful social interactions between patrons at the library through the introduction of new programs
  13| such as book clubs, open mics, craft circles, or support groups.
  14| Children’s Service Notes
  15|    We learned of our L S T A Grant Award which will arrive in FY2023 and will be used in the Children’s Area to
  16|     provide STEM programming for children ages 4-11. We are also creating STEM project kits for circulation. These
  17|     kits will be available for patron and classroom use.
  18|    We purchased and installed the BEAM projector system, thanks to the Friends of the Lunenburg Public Library.
  19|     Something you don’t often see in libraries, especially those our size, this interactive learning game system comes
  20|     with 100 games designed to engage and entertain players of all ages. The games provide education reinforcement
  21|     and highly social activities.
  22|    We added two new Platinum all-in-one computer workstations from AWE Learning. These computers are
  23|     specifically designed to provide educational resources and reinforcement activities for elementary aged students.
  24|    We offered 117 programs for children, attended by 2,409 individuals. The include storytimes, book clubs, Lego
  25|     nights, and so many more!
  26|    We added a raised bed Children’s Garden. This garden features a variety of herbs and plants chosen to provide
  27|     sensory and educational experiences for library patrons of all ages.
  28|    We completed a successful Summer Reading Program to help bridge the gap between school years. We registered
  29|     221 children as participants, and offered 57 programs which were attended by 1240 children and family members
  30|     during the program.
  31| Children’s Service Goals for FY23 & Beyond
  32| 1.  Encourage children and families to use the library from an early age as this will make them more likely to remain
  33|     users in future years.
  34| 2.  Continue to provide programs especially geared toward engaging students in grades 4-6.
  35| 3.  Add new book club for kids in grades 2-3 based on “Choose Your Own Adventure Series.”
  36| 4.  Provide STEM programs and circulating STEM kits for children ages 5-11.

   8|          have been offering 4-week programs twice a year.

  10|          library is busier than ever as a community hub that teens are. They come to hang out with friends, use

  14|     1. Continue to try out different configurations in the Teen Room to make the best use of the space and to

  22| funding to support STEM learning for all ages during FY2023. We hope to bring programming, circulating
  23| kits, learning guides, and more to people of all ages with this funding. Additionally, having secured funding
  24| for two additional staff members, we anticipate opening our doors to the public Friday afternoons, increasing
  25| the number of hours during the week that the public can access the library by 11%. These big changes will
  26| positively impact the library’s ability to serve our community and enable us to reach groups of residents who
  27| might not normally have the opportunity to engage with library services. We will plan and implement outreach
  28| services and an expanded program plan to continue to deliver these important core library services. We plan
  29| for the library to continue to be a vital, thriving location where people of all ages can come to meet, learn, and
  30| have fun. Come visit the library soon!

   2| LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   3| FY22  Mission: Establish, develop, modernize and operate the Town of Lunenburg’s
   4| Public, Government, and Educational (PEG) Access Channels to reflect what matters to
   5| Lunenburg
   6| Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   7| Steve Walker, Chair
   8| Pete Lincoln, Vice Chair
   9| Steve Archambault, Secretary
  10| Faith Beall, Member
  11| Paul Sarapas, Member
  12| The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the Public
  13| Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town’s only cable-service provider.
  14| The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-broadcast
  15| programming from both town, school functions and meetings, as well as programming from publicly available sources.
  16| Programming
  17| CH9 - Public
  18| Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 63% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY22, down 7% from FY21.  The total
  19| number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (outside resources), decreased from 91 in FY21 to 82
  20| programs in FY22.
  21| The 52 local programs included high school sports, band concerts, school events and featured projects. The remaining 30
  22| (37%) programs were obtained through shared media sources.
  23| Channel 9 – Public Channel
  24| Several categories of local programming were aired on Ch9. Among the top programs were Informational 28%, Events
  25| 23% and Entertainment 22%, making up the majority 63% of Ch9 programming (see chart, below).
  26| Two of the top three program categories, Informational and Events (51%) provided viewers with presentations and
  27| overviews that helped to familiarize them with resources and information, as well as current events happening around
  28| town.

===PAGE 70=== (layout, 31 lines)
   1| Locally produced programs and government meetings are available on the LunenburgAccess YouTube Channel.  http://
   2| www.youtube.com/LunenburgAccess
   3| Links to the YouTube channel for local Ch9 programs can be found on Lunenburg Public Access                        ’ Facebook Page
   4|  https://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable
   5| CH8 – Government
   6| Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally broadcast meetings.  In addition to several live weekly
   7| municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other examples of government programs downloaded from MassAccess
   8| (massaccess.telvue.com), include Massachusetts State Agency PSAs and programs of a political nature.  There were also
   9| additional programs from other access stations added, including the Kushmeric Connection from Fitchburg Public
  10| Access.
  11| FY22 brought a slight increase in Government programming with more committee meeting and event recording requests.
  12| The additional programming allowed residents to view meetings they normally wouldn’t see.  And during this time, PAC
  13|  also improved the recording of Town Meeting using 4 cameras, which increased the overall quality of the broadcast.
  14| A few committees and boards continue to use Zoom only, (not live broadcast) and record meetings to the Cloud. These
  15| meetings are uploaded from the Cloud to one of the Lunenburg Public Access YouTube channels (LunenburgAccess) for
  16| later viewing. This has resulted in easy access to a greater number of board and committee meetings.
  17| CH99    – Educational
  18| In May of FY20, PAC added an Educational Channel, Comcast Channel 99.  The additional channel has allowed PAC to
  19| feature local educational content to viewers in addition to the bi-monthly School Committee meetings.  As of FY22,
  20| most of the channel’s content remains to be provided by download resources, including programs such as “Auto Sense”,
  21|  “Fur, Fins and Feathers”, and “Chess Chat.”
  22| PAC continues to work toward content growth on this channel.
  23|                                                                                  As a member of the MassAccess organization, PAC
  24|                                                                                  is given access to the Telvue Connect
  25|                                                                                  (massaccess.telvue.com) database for sharing
  26| programming.  Many shows downloaded and aired on Channel 9 and Ch99 are sourced from this outlet, including productions of both state and nationwide
  27| “Democracy NOW”, “Off the Shelf”, and “In the Toybox. “
  28| In FY20, PAC began sharing their own locally produced programs with the MassAccess content database.  The results
  29| were outstanding!  In FY22, Lunenburg’s “Talkin’ Baseball” episodes were, and continue to be, downloaded by over 60
  30| media centers around the country.  And, “Storytime – The Books  We Love” was downloaded by over 70 public access
  31|                                                                       70

===PAGE 71=== (layout, 35 lines)
   1| stations mostly located in Massachusetts but reaching as far as California.  We are delighted to showcase our programs
   2| to the community while using the database to add to PAC content.
   3| In addition to MassAccess Telvue downloads, PAC also receives local programs from nearby access stations that air on
   4| Ch9, including “Barbara and You”, “Chess Chat” and “Wicked Good Food.”
   5| PAC Manager, staff and committee members regularly attend MassAccess conferences and Meet & Greets to develop
   6| relationships and resources with other media centers.
   7| Studio and Equipment
   8| PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013.  We encourage the public to take advantage of the
   9| facilities and equipment offered for creative productions. The PAC manager also supports various town departments in
  10| the use of public access’ resources for their communication needs.
  11| In FY22 PAC made a major investment adding new studio set lighting.  With the addition of professional lighting,
  12| programs saw a drastic increase in quality.   PAC videographers were trained on the use of the new lighting and have the
  13| experience and know-how to provide professional quality programming.
  14| As the need for multi-camera productions has increased, PAC added a new AV MATRIX portable switcher.  This
  15| switcher was used on location for Town Meetings as well as sports and events.  It replaced the outdated switcher which
  16| was complicated and cumbersome.
  17| Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  18| In FY22, there was one full-time Manager and 5 part-time contract videographers and a contracted technical consultant.
  19| There were openings for 2 part-time videographers.
  20| The PAC Manager administers the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all production and programming
  21| schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, studio and inventory administration, advertising, social
  22| media and equipment and system troubleshooting.  Videographers provided video recording of committee meetings and
  23| additional local video assignments and projects.
  24| A technical contract consultant worked with the PAC manager to provided technical and training assistance as needed.
  25| It is expected that this contract will continue through FY23.  However, PAC searched for, and has the funds budgeted to
  26| hire a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the staff in FY23.
  27| PAC is always looking for producers.  If a community member has a special skill or interest, PAC will help make the
  28| show happen.  Our studio and staff are equipped to assist new producers in video production.
  29| Much effort has gone into recruiting volunteers, with little success.
  30| Financials
  31| PAC received its income from Comcast in quarterly payments (received August, November, February and May) and
  32| monthly interest payments deposited into the Enterprise Fund. The overall revenue received for FY22 from Comcast was
  33| $199,956.37 staying flat from $199,736.30 (+0 .11 % over FY21).  The budget starting balance was set at $220,866 for
  34| FY22 projected expenses and capital costs.

   3| practice.  Zoom Hybrid meetings continue to be administered with new on-location Zoom Hybrid environments being
   4| planned for boards and committees.  PAC continued to administer these meetings and has adapted to the new
   5| technology.  Hybrid meetings have allowed PAC to air more Government meetings than ever before.
   6| While overall programming did not increase over FY21, PAC made a few key equipment improvements during this
   7| period.  The addition of a new camera switcher made an impact on the production of on-location broadcasts and multi-

   9| lighting has been a “game-changer” bringing our programs to a more professional level.  These upgrades will pave the
  10| way for better productions and content in FY23.

  17|                             SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
  18|                                              JANUARY 1, 2022 – NOVEMBER 1, 2022
  19| The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is administered by the Assistant Town Manager/HR Director.
  20| What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off?
  21| The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to have an
  22| abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing jobs such as general
  23| office work, custodial, library shelving, meals on wheels, school assistance, etc., to earn the tax abatement.
  24| The 2022 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to the participant’s 3rd quarter real estate tax bill. The current
  25| hourly rate is $15.00 per hour.
  26| Eligibility Requirements
  27|    Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  28|    Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1             st
  29|    Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse          ’s name (60 years+)
  30|    Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):
  31|          one person household - $50,000
  32|          two person household - $75,000
  33|    Earnings are exempt from state taxes
  34|    Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Retirement
  35|     or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement
  36|    Final amount earned will be applied to the 3         rd quarter real estate tax
  37|    All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check
  38|    All Property Tax Workers must re- apply and be accepted annually for participation in the program
  39| 2022 Participation
  40| During 2022, there were eight residents who participated in the Program in the following departments:
  41|    Building Department/Zoning
  42|                                                                        73

  12|                                              SEWER COMMISSION – FY22
  13| The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the future, and
  14| developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town’s Charter, responsibility for the day-to-
  15| day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager who
  16| delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational side of the system to the Department of Public
  17| Works Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 86% of Lunenburg
  18| sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 14% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  19| In FY22 the following members served on the Sewer Commission; Dave MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair,
  20| Brett Ramsden-Clerk and Members Mike Deveikis and Mike Nault. The Commission continued to meet virtually via
  21| Zoom for all meetings and voted to begin each meeting with the Pledge of Allegiance.
  22| There are currently 915 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 327 which have access to Town
  23| Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY22, 14 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The Commission billed out
  24| $1,021,525 in quarterly sewer bills and had $30,593 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners’ property tax bills
  25| for non-payment.
  26| During FY22, CTE (Chad Empey) continued to provide excellent service for the Operation & Maintenance of the Sewer
  27| Stations. The Commission approved funds for repairs to the Dana Street Station which included the replacement of the
  28| impellers, seals and gaskets and approved the replacement of the electrical panel at the Mass 2 station.  These upgrades
  29| are important to keep the stations running smoothly. The Commission voted 3/8/22 to approve the Year 3 contract with
  30| CTE, with a 4% increase to commence 7/1/22.
  31| DPW Director Rob Oliva worked with the Commission in FY22 to implement the Maintenance Plan for the sewer
  32| system. Clean Basins was contracted to camera and auger a blockage on Mass Ave. near Riley Road. In October 2021
  33| Green Mountain Pipe grouted the pipe joints on Elizabeth St. to address a long               -standing Inflow/Infiltration (I/I) issue in
  34| 3 services. Rob unfortunately left Lunenburg in March 2022, but the Commission welcomed Bill Bernard in May as
  35| Director.
  36|                                                                       74

===PAGE 75=== (layout, 39 lines)
   1| SEWER COMMISSION – FY22
   2| Bill has been working diligently with the Commission to carry out the many Maintenance Plan projects in process, such
   3| as jetting, manhole repairs and overseeing maintenance at the pump stations.
   4| In March 2022 the Commission approved hiring an Assistant Business Manager, to share the increasing demands of the
   5| Business Manager’s job.  Paula Bertram was hired to assist the Business Manager, Barb Lefebvre with billing, account
   6| management and other office activities.
   7| In FY22, the Commission continued the annual permit program for the 20 Food Service Establishments with grease traps
   8| and interceptors on Town Sewer. The Commission once again voted to waive the Annual Permit Renewal Fee for the
   9| Grease Trap Program, as many restaurants were still struggling to gain back business after the Covid 19 closures. In
  10| February 2022, the Commission voted to drop the Annual Permit Renewal fees for establishments in compliance,
  11| effective with FY23 renewals.  Those who are not in compliance will pay $350 for internal traps and $650 for external
  12| trap renewals.  Grease Trap compliance continues to be a focus of the Commission as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can
  13| negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the sewer system.  While the Grease Trap Regulation
  14| focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease
  15| into the system which increases everyone’s costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  16| While working on the FY23 budget, an 8% rate increase was approved by the Commission to cover increasing costs in
  17| usage from Fitchburg and Leominster. The rate increased from $12.65/100 cu.ft. to $13.66/100 cu.ft. and the minimum
  18| charge from $107.00 to $115.00/quarter.  The Commission continued working in FY22 with Flow Assessment to
  19| maintain and ensure the accuracy of the flow meters that measure usage going to Fitchburg and Leominster.
  20| The Commission also voted to update several policies for re-connection fees, prorating bills for change of ownership or
  21| new connections, easements and waivers. A public hearing will be held in FY23 to address the policy updates.
  22| The Commission met with several groups in FY22 looking to expand sewer. The Parks Commission requested to expand
  23| the Sewer Service Area (SSA) to include Marshall Park, which was approved at the November 2021 Town Meeting.
  24| Extensions for Upland Ave./Eastern Ave. and White St./Maple Parkway were discussed and preliminary designs, with
  25| estimates, were done by Wright Pierce. However, a survey of residents did not indicate the necessary 2/3rds interest in
  26| favor of the extensions. Other projects before the Commission  were an 8-unit condo at 711 Mass. Ave., lots  at 790-792
  27| Mass. Ave., and a marijuana establishment (Uma Flowers) at 43 Summer St.  A horse rescue facility and 20-unit
  28| residential project at 5 Chestnut St. requested to be added to the SSA. An article was added to the May Town Meeting
  29| but was passed over. The 200 apartment 40B project application on Howard Street was approved by Mass Housing to
  30| proceed in seeking approvals from the Lunenburg ZBA.  The project will seek a large expansion of the Sewer Service
  31| Area and Water District to bring utilities to the project.
  32| At the 5/7/22 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 4 articles, 3 of which related to the FY23 budget and
  33| stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY23, $853,112.53 was appropriated from Sewer Enterprise
  34| revenues, $20,000.00 transferred from the Sewer Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund, $20,962.00 was transferred from
  35| the Sewer Reserve Capacity Fund and the sum of $319,107.47 was appropriated from Retained Earnings for a total
  36| appropriation of $1,213,182.00 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY23, including the amount of $793,968.47
  37| approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund’s share of Town services and debt service on the sewer betterments. The
  38| second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $35,548.86, approximately
  39|                                                                        75

===PAGE 77=== (layout, 44 lines)
   1|                                                    VETERANS’  SERVICES
   2| The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has the most comprehensive system of assistance offered to Veterans,
   3| families, and surviving spouses in all of the United States.  Every city or town in Massachusetts, in accordance
   4| with chapter 115 of the Massachusetts General Laws, must have available to its citizens a Veterans’ Services
   5| Officer (VSO), either full- or part-time, as determined by the community population, in order to serve the
   6| needs of its Veterans.  Lunenburg has a part          -time VSO at the Ritter Building at 960 Massachusetts Ave.
   7| Working with the Town census of 2021, shows Lunenburg in excess of 618 residents who claimed either
   8| military service or veteran status.  On a monthly basis, the veterans and widows of veterans in Lunenburg
   9| receive in excess of $328,330.74 in federal benefits (Veterans Administration), which they mostly put back
  10| into the community by paying their taxes and through discretionary spending.
  11| Veterans, families, or widows who qualify can be provided monthly financial assistance (reimbursements) for
  12| specific living expenses such as heating fuel, medical care, and pharmaceutical co                   -payments.  With the VSO’s
  13| approval, the Town of Lunenburg provides the financial assistance and is then reimbursed by the state’s
  14| department of Veterans’ Services (DVS).  It is the function of the Veterans                 ’ Services Office to identify
  15| qualifying members of the community and assist with enrollment into the DVS program.  Additionally, certain
  16| Veterans and/or widows may also be eligible for federal benefits from the Veterans Administration (VA).
  17| Only Massachusetts offers both of these services to every community.
  18| During Fiscal Year 2022 (July 2021          - June 2022), Lunenburg served an average of 10 recipients (Veterans,
  19| families, or widows) per month.  Lunenburg paid out $64,435.43 in benefits, or an average of $5,369.62 per
  20| recipient per month.  Lunenburg was reimbursed $48,326.57 by DVS, which is 75% of what was given as
  21| assistance, the maximum allowable share.
  22| Some examples of Veterans’ Service Office assistance services include:
  23|    Enrollment in chapter 115, State Veterans benefits
  24|    Assistance obtaining military service records or replacement medals/decorations
  25|    Tuition waivers at Massachusetts state colleges
  26|    Housing assistance, including Veterans Shelters and Veterans Homes
  27|    Payments of “Welcome Home” bonuses for deployed Veterans
  28|    Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates
  29|    Employment assistance
  30| The Veterans’ Service Office also provides information, advice, and assistance to any citizen interested in
  31| learning about their qualifications for federal benefits, such as:
  32|    VA disability payments and property tax exemptions
  33|    Tax-exempt annuities for parents or spouses of those killed in Action or those who are declared 100%
  34|     disabled from service-connected conditions.
  35|    VA pensions, Aid and Attendance assistance, and death benefits
  36|    VA medical care for both physical and mental needs
  37|    Vocational and Educational opportunities (the GI Bill)
  38|    VA Loans
  39|    Burial Assistance and Grave Markers (Veterans or Civilian cemeteries)
  40| Respectfully submitted,
  41| David Lauzon Jr.
  42| Interim VSO
  43| Office phone 978-582-4176

   1|                                                        BOARD OF HEALTH
   2| The Board of Health Office is located in the Ritter Building on the corner of Mass. Ave and Lancaster Ave. Our office is
   3| on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.  We can be reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.
   4| The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its mission to
   5| maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2022, reviewing Title V Inspection Reports, building
   6| permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public health
   7| related issues as well as resident concerns.
   8| In addition, our local Board continued to be tasked with responding to the emerging COVID-19 pandemic.  The Board of
   9| Health assisted residents and businesses in understanding ever-changing guidance and compliance with those orders.  We
  10| continued to conducted compliance checks and responded to complaints.  As the pandemic continued, our office assisted
  11| residents and businesses in understanding testing, quarantine and necessary compliance.  Through Nashoba Associated
  12| Boards of Health, regional Covid Vaccine Clinics were held.  Wash hands frequently, wear a mask, and keep six feet rule.
  13| Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing.  Meetings continue between our agents, other
  14| Town Departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our
  15| region.
  16| This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, repairs or
  17| upgrades to septic systems, well permits and food service permits.  The Board continues its vigilance with regard to
  18| septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect the
  19| environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  20| The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba Associated
  21| Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, nursing services is provided to our resident.  This year,
  22| clinics provided our residents with inoculations for Flu as well as Senior well adult clinics.
  23| The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project.  Mosquito Control monitors the
  24| mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays
  25| frequently to control the population during the warmer times of the year.  Residents who want to have their property
  26| sprayed can do so by emailing to:  cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage
  27| at:  http://www.cmmcp.org , or calling the Board of Health office.  This service is free of charge to residents.
  28| With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to ill residents recovering from illness and
  29| Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  30| Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  31| Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of Lunenburg.
  32| If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board office at 582-4146
  33| ext. 430.
  34|                                NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH
  35| Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the Lunenburg Board of Health in meeting the challenges of
  36| environmental and public health needs in your community. As the pandemic specific requirements and guidance were eased,
  37| and the State of Emergency ended in June, we have provided assistance to your Board’s return to pre-pandemic public health
  38| activities.
  39|     The agency staff informed, educated and stayed current with the COVID-19 information from the Department of Public
  40|      Health to help residents in your community navigate the changes.
  41|     Our public health nursing staff were still providing contact tracing and outreach in your communities (including regional
  42|      COVID-19 vaccination clinics), while reaching out to Councils on Aging to establish well adult clinics and conduct
  43|      regular fall flu clinics.
  44|     Our VNA continued their home health activities in your community.
  45|     As a recipient of a Public Health Excellence Grant, we have brough on staff to increase our presence in food service
  46|      establishments and provide improved communication, and public health education to the Nashoba District..
  47| We look forward to continuing our work with Lunenburg's Board of Health to meet the public health, environmental and

===PAGE 83=== (layout, 28 lines)
   1| Community Health- Communications and Public Health Education Summary
   2| The Public Health Educator/Communications Specialist (PHE/CS) role has evolved since its inception in Sept 2021 to
   3| best support the needs of the District . In the past year, the PHE/CS has been communicating timely and relevant public
   4| health information to towns and stakeholders, and providing educational opportunities across the District with a social
   5| determinants of health (SDOH) lens.  This work has included:
   6|    Developing educational programs and presentations both independently and collaboratively with the PH nursing
   7|     team- Harm reduction, emergency preparedness, etc.
   8|    Providing education and advocacy related to treatment and de-stigmatization of substance use disorder
   9|    Maintaining up-to-date distribution lists, communicating within the district via channels such as quarterly
  10|     newsletters, regular email updates, etc. pertaining to public health news, information and events such as flu and
  11|     rabies clinics.  Exploring ways to diversify communication methods to reach as many community members as
  12|     possible.
  13|    Building and maintaining relationships with stakeholders through meetings and attending community events to
  14|     represent NABH
  15|    Serving as a resource for community members with public health related questions across a broad range of topics;
  16|     developing a robust, in-depth Resources page for the website (which is currently being finalized)
  17|    Attending trainings with an emphasis on health equity and public health service regionalization.  MDPH has
  18|     prioritized these areas because research has shown they have the biggest impact on health outcomes for the broadest
  19|     range of people.
  20|    PHE/CS has utilized an SDOH lens to identify health inequities that most impact the NABH region to focus efforts
  21|     and collaborate with community partners to lessen these gaps.
  22|    Developing educational and communications materials for district and individual towns by request including print,
  23|     electronic, presentation, and TV/news media materials
  24|    Maintaining and improving upon NABH website to keep info relevant and accessible. Maintaining agency social
  25|     media to reach more people in district with current, relevant and seasonal public health information.
  26|    Organizing events to facilitate the exchange of information such as a Spring Wellness Festival and first regional in
  27|     formal local board of health gathering

===PAGE 84=== (layout, 49 lines)
   1|                                                       FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2| The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
   3|          In 2022, your Fire Department responded to 1990 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   4|          Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions                                  385                 Emergency Medical      1143
   5|          Inspections                                                           446                 Other                             16
   6| This represents a 14% increase in emergency calls for service over 2021, mostly in Emergency Medical calls.
   7|          The Department consists of 10 Career and 30-35 On Call/Per Diem Firefighters and Emergency Medical
   8| Technicians, plus a full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator.  Three Firefighter/EMT or Paramedics are on
   9| duty 24/7 at the Station.  This coverage is provided by a combination of our Career Staff and Per Diem Firefighters.
  10| Staffing the Station with dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency
  11| occurs.  As many calls require more personnel than are on duty, we continue to rely heavily on the dedication and
  12| commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians.
  13|          After nearly five years of preparation, the Department is in the final stages of licensing to provide our own
  14| Paramedic Service.  Firefighter Olivia Hayes certified as a Paramedic in October 2022 and Firefighter Melissa Buchanan
  15| will be obtaining her certification in January 2023. This brings us to four Fulltime Firefighter/Paramedics with an
  16| additional eight On Call/per diem Firefighter/Paramedics.  In addition, Firefighter Jennie Smith will begin training as a
  17| Paramedic in January of 2023.  Paramedic training is in excess of 2000 hours and requires anywhere from 9                            -18 months
  18| of  college  level  classroom  education, clinical  time  and  field  internships  to  obtain  certification.   The  Department
  19| continues to train our newly hired full time staff to this level and recruit licensed Paramedics to fill Career positions
  20| when possible.  We will begin providing Paramedic Level care to the community in January 2023.
  21|          In 2022, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 446 Inspections.   This is in addition to multiple fire drills and
  22| public education initiatives.  By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations
  23| when a home is sold.  We are also required to inspect all new oil burner and propane gas installations.  Any business
  24| holding a liquor license is also requires a yearly inspection for fire safety issues.  In addition, all new construction
  25| projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction
  26| process.  Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2022 include:
  27|    Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety Education in
  28|     both the Schools and with Senior Citizens.  This Grant provides funding for both materials and personnel to bring
  29|     fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.
  30|    Participation in the Senior Center’s “Sand for Seniors” Program.  This program provides buckets of walkway sand
  31|     for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months.  In partnership with this
  32|     program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for Smoke
  33|     and Carbon Monoxide Detectors.  If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to install free detectors
  34|     obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  35|    It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke detectors.
  36| Academy    ’s Call/Volunteer Recruit Training Program in June of 2022.  This 240Firefighters Samuel Johnson and Timothy O’Connell graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting -hour program trains new personnel in
  37| the basics of firefighting and hazardous materials response.  The program is a requirement for any new Firefighters with
  38| the Department.
  39|          Eight Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive
  40| Teams.  These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such as Confined Space, Rescue from
  41| Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery.  In conjunction with
  42| these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer.
  43| 10 years old.  The Ambulances continue to see increased wear and tear as we see not only increased calls for service, but Apparatus replacement has reached a plateau for a few years, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck all under
  44| longer transport times to other hospitals and critical care facilities.  It should be noted that 30 years ago, we had four
  45| hospitals within a 10-15 minute transport time. Today there are two.  In addition, many cardiac and trauma patients
  46| require  definitive  treatment  in  specialty  facilities, often  requiring  transports  to  Worcester.    Given  long  delays  in
  47| obtaining new units, a replacement for Rescue 1, our 2014 ambulance has been moved up two years and is being
  48| requested on the FY’24 Capital Plan.
  49|                                                                         84

===PAGE 85=== (plain, 32 lines)
   1| 85
   2| FIRE DEPARTMENT
   3| The Turnout Gear Replacement program that was begun under the Capital Budget last year has been completed.
   4| All personnel now have state of the art protective clothing, with a serviceable spare set of equipment as well.
   5| Firefighting protective clothing must be cleaned after every fire to rid it of cancer causing materials that are often
   6| byproducts of today’s synthetic materials burning.  As washing and drying firefighting cloth can take from 24 -48 hours,
   7| having a spare set available allows all personnel to remain “in service” at all time.
   8| Replacement of our outdated Forest Fire protective clothing with a dual -purpose type of clothing that is suitable
   9| for both brush fires and motor vehicle/technical rescue situations is still underway.  This project is funded through a
  10| State Grant for Safety Equipment.  Though supply chain issues have slowed the process, we anticipate having the
  11| equipment by spring of 2023.  A State Firefighting Safety Grant was also received in 2022.  This will replace several
  12| hundred feet of fire hose and some outdated nozzles and adapters.  This grant is expected to be completed sometime in
  13| 2023.
  14| In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding
  15| commitment to the Town.  Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress me every day.  In
  16| addition, I would like to express my gratitude to the members of the Department who have helped make the Paramedic
  17| Program a reality.  From those who helped develop policies and those two helped procure equipment, to those members
  18| who endured the challenges of Paramedic training.  Your efforts have helped bring about a massive improvement in
  19| service to our residents.  My thanks also to the Town Manager for her unwavering support of this vital project.
  20| I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help.  The
  21| level of interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business in invisible to many people, but it
  22| is critical to all operations.  Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing support of
  23| public safety.  Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have come to expect from us.

   8|      Mission Statement:
   9|      We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services
  10|      to our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the benefits that
  11|      are realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed
  12|      with our community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship. We will strive to deliver
  13|      police services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all
  14|      individual rights. We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend
  15|      criminal offenders, and help resolve quality of life issues.

   6| Department Review:
   7| It is an honor to again provide the Lunenburg Police Department’s Annual report as the Chief of Police. 2022 continued
   8| to be a challenging year for the Police Department as the community came out of the grips of Covid                         –19. We have also
   9| faced the challenges associated with the economy, population growth, and increased traffic within the community. A
  10| nationwide trend of recruiting and retaining Police Officers continue to have an effect on our Department. Although
  11| faced with these challenges, the Department remains committed to being pro- active, and strives to make the Community
  12|  safe for all.
  13| K9 Jerry will be retiring at the end of the fiscal year. Jerry has been with the Department since 2014, and has performed
  14| admirably throughout his career. Jerry will remain with Sgt. Tocci, who has agreed to keep him through his retirement
  15| years. We wish Jerry good health and a long retirement. Job well done Jerry.
  16| Off. Nicholas Marino accepted a position with the Acton Police Department. We thank Nick for his service, and wish
  17| him the best of luck with Acton PD.
  18| Reserve Officer/Desk Officer Lisa Melnicki has accepted a position in the private sector. We thank Lisa for her service,
  19| and wish her the best of luck in her future endeavors.
  20| Off. Jesse Reed was hired to the full times ranks. We are happy to welcome a veteran police officer from Berlin Police
  21| Department who will bring his experience and knowledge to the Department.
  22| Off. Deven O    ’Brien has returned to the Department as a full time police officer. Off. O’Brien started his career with our
  23| Department as a Reserve Officer in 2013 and in 2014 was appointed as a full time police officer. In 2018 Off. O’Brien
  24| accepted a position with the Marlborough Police Department. We were happy to welcome Off. O’Brien back to the
  25| Department, and continue his service where his Law Enforcement career began.
  26| Off. Paul Theodoulou was hired to the full time ranks after serving a short period time as a Reserve Officer with the
  27| Department. Off. Theodoulou is a veteran police officer from Fitchburg Police Department as well Townsend Police
  28| Department. Off. Theodoulou brings a wealth of experience and knowledge to our Department. We were fortunate to
  29|  have landed Off. Theodoulou.
  30| Off. George “Trey” Tibbetts was elevated to a full time position after serving a short period of time as a Reserve Officer
  31| with the Department. Off. Tibbetts was added to the Department in accordance with the Department’s 10 year hiring
  32| plan. Trey completed his training in a relatively short period of time, and we are excited to watch him grow with the
  33| Department.
  34| As we became fully staffed we anticipated filling our vacant positions. The first and foremost was filling a vacant
  35| Sergeant’s position that would provide us much needed supervision for the Patrol Bureau. We also anticipated filling a
  36| vacant Detective’s position, as well as adding an additional School Resource/Community Outreach Officer.
  37| Sgt. John Morreale was promoted to fill the vacant Sergeant’s position. Sgt. Morreale has been with the Department
  38| since 2014 when he began his career as a Reserve Officer, and has worked up through the ranks as he has progressed in
  39| his career. Sgt. Morreale has been assigned as the Department’s Accreditation Manager as well as the split shift patrol
  40|  supervisor.
  41| Det. Joshua Mathieu was elevated to the Detective position. Det. Mathieu started with the Department in 2011 and has
  42| risen through the ranks to his current position. Det. Mathieu brings a wealth of experience, knowledge and training to the
  43| position. Det. Mathieu is an excellent investigator, and will be a great addition to the Investigative Bureau.
  44| On October 26, 2022 the Department was certified through the Massachusetts Police Accreditation Commission
  45| (MPAC), which is recognized as a best practice organization. Through the tireless work of Sgt. Morreale, and the
  46| cooperation and collaboration with members of our Department we were able to complete the first phase of
  47| accreditation. The Department was recognized and received the MPAC award on October 26, 2022 at the Commission’s
  48|                                                                          88

   1| award ceremony held that Marlborough Country Club. We look forward to an accelerated process and becoming fully
   2| accredited by the summer of 2023.
   3| We continue to work towards a Co-Response Model approach to mental health type of calls. This would involve
   4| partnering officer(s) with a mental health clinician to assist with individuals in crisis. We have been meeting with
   5| Community Health Link and Advocates to assess our needs, and develop a Co-Response plan. We have also been
   6| exploring a partnership with the Town of Lancaster to share a clinician between the towns. We hope to have this
   7| program become a reality by the fall of 2023.

  31|                                   Attempted/Threat of Suicide                               29
  32|                                   Mental Health Evaluations                                 40
  33|                         Firearms Licensing totals:                                          222
  34|                                   LTC Class A:                                              210
  35|                                   FID:                                                      11
  36|                                   Sell Ammunition                                           1
  37|                         Animal Control totals:                                              205
  38|                                   Dog Bites:                                                11

===PAGE 90=== (layout, 54 lines)
   1|   As result of the above calls our officers generated a total of 1,777 written reports. There were 434 reports
   2|   involving Incident Based Reporting (IBR), and National Incident Based Reporting (NIBRS), which is a State and
   3|   Federal Crime reporting data base. The Officers also generated an additional 483 written reports that did not fall
   4|   into the IBR/NIBRS category.
   5|   According to NIBRS, reported crimes are broken down into three categories, Crimes Against Persons, Crimes Against
   6|   Property, and Crimes Against Society. These are further broken down into Group A and Group B categories to closely
   7|   match the Uniform Crime Reporting (UCR) program. The Lunenburg Police Department regularly reports IBR
   8|   information to the State, which in turn reports the information to the Federal Bureau of Investigation.
   9|   In 2022 there has been a 19% increase in Group A, Crimes Against Persons, with Aggravated and Simple Assaults
  10|   as well as Intimidation (witness/victim) accounting for the majority of the incidents with 39 out of 62 total reported
  11|   incidents. These numbers appear to have a direct correlation to the Domestic Violence calls that officers have
  12|   responded to in 2022.
  13|   In 2022 there has been a 34% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Property. Although we have observed a
  14|   decrease in property crimes, the area we have observed an increase is shoplifting, accounting for a majority of the
  15|   incidents with 40 out of 142 total reported incidents.
  16| Training:
  17| Training continues to be a priority for the Department. The Department’s in-house trainers look for ingenuitive ways to
  18| complement our annual in-service training. In addition, several officers have attended specialized training in various
  19| areas of Law Enforcement as a part of professional development.
  20| Use of Force:
  21| The Department conducts an annual analysis of Use of Force incidents, as well as review(s) of the incidents as they
  22| occur. In 2022 officers were involved in nine (9) use of force incidents, which remained the same as compared to 2021.
  23| In each Use of Force incident de-escalation tactics, including the use of verbal tactics, were first utilized prior to any Use
  24| of Force application. Officers continue to strive to de-escalate confrontations prior to any application of Use of Force.
  25| Motor Vehicle Pursuits:
  26| The Department conducts an annual analysis of Motor Vehicle Pursuits, as well as review(s) of pursuits as they occur. In
  27| 2022 officers were involved in five (5) motor vehicle pursuits, which remained the same as compared to 2021. One
  28| pursuit resulted in a motor vehicle accident but occurred after the pursuit had been terminated. No injuries to the public,
  29|  officers or suspect(s) involved in the pursuits were reported.
  30| Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District:
  31| The Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District (NVRDD) is a Regional Emergency Communications Center located in
  32| Devens, MA. NVRDD serves as the 9-1-1 public safety answering point and dispatch center for Police, Fire and EMS
  33| for the communities of Lunenburg, Bolton, Devens, Harvard, Lancaster and Lunenburg. NVRDD was one of the first
  34| Regional Emergency Communications Centers in the State of Massachusetts, opening its doors on June 18, 2013.
  35| NVRDD serves a total population of just over 35,000 residents and thousands more visitors to the orchards, farms,
  36| businesses, and attractions within its six communities. In 2022, NVRDD handled 33,500 phone calls coming in on 911
  37| and business lines. NVRDD also coordinated the response to over 89,900 calls for service. NVRDD's staff are highly
  38| skilled in call-handling and dispatching, and are all able to perform both functions simultaneously. These first of the first
  39| -responders provide a vital and seamless link between the community and their local police, fire, and emergency medical
  40| services. While the focus is on handling emergencies, our staff also serves the public and our responders by providing
  41| other  administrative  and  non-emergency  dispatch  tasks.  NVRDD  staffs  the  communications  center  with  multiple
  42|  dispatchers and supervisors each shift to handle calls and requests.
  43| In 2022 NVRDD processed a total of 89,900 calls for service (CFS). Of these calls for service,
  44| 32,067 were for the Town of Lunenburg. These calls for service average out to 2,673 CFS per
  45| month in the Town of and 88 CFS per day in the Town of Lunenburg. Each CFS represents one
  46| report entered into the Computer Aided Dispatch system by the dispatchers for the 9-1-1 calls
  47| handled, the police, fire, or medical emergencies dispatched, and the other miscellaneous duties
  48| they perform on a daily basis.
  49| In 2022 NVRDD Telecommunicators answered 10,989 9-1-1 Calls. This total averages out to 916
  50| emergency 9- 1-1 calls per month and approximately 30 emergency 911 calls per day. NVRDD Staff prides itself in
  51| customer service and standards of care. Our average 9-1-1 answer time is 3.5 seconds, which is approximately half of a
  52| normal telephone “ring”. Our staff is also evaluated monthly on their call handling skills and they rate at an average 94%
  53| adherence to the call taking protocols.
  54|                                                                         90

   5| Full-Time Positions filled, and all 3 Part-Time Positions filled. NVRDD is projected to be fully staffed by the
   6| beginning of FY24.
   7| Conclusion:
   8| I would like to thank the Citizens of Lunenburg for their continued support. Without collaboration with our

  12| Community, and the work that they perform on a daily basis. The personnel of the Department play a vital role in
  13| making Lunenburg a great place to live, work and visit for all.

   1|                                                        PLANNING BOARD
   2| Fourteen (14) ANR Plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law, but still
   3|  requiring Board endorsement) were approved.
   4| Lunenburg’s Stormwater Bylaw requires Board review and approval for any development/redevelopment project that
   5| disturbs one acre or more of land.  This Bylaw safeguards the public health, safety, welfare and the environment by
   6| minimizing adverse impacts of contaminated stormwater both off-site and downstream.  These updates are more likely to
   7| affect large commercial projects and multi-family dwellings; not the average homeowner wanting to put in a shed,
   8| garden, pool, etc.  The Board approved stormwater permits for the following:
   9|              A warehouse off Leominster-Shirley Road;
  10|              A single-family house lot at 324 Flat Hill Road;
  11|              Construction of a 7-bay garage at 225 & 259 Leominster-Shirley Road;
  12|              The creation of four (4) lots at Cross and Mulpus;
  13|              The creation of six (6) lots on the corner of Holman and Northfield Road.
  14| Lunenburg has four designated Scenic Roads; Lancaster Avenue, Flat Hill Road, Northfield Road and Leominster Road.
  15| Over the past year you may have noticed building lots, such as some of those mentioned in the paragraph above, being
  16| created on some of these roads.  As these are Scenic Roads, any repair, maintenance, re-construction, or paving work in
  17| the Town’s right-of-way cannot involve the cutting or removal of trees or the tearing down or destruction of stone walls
  18| without consent of the Planning Board.
  19| The Commonwealth issued Site Eligibility for a proposed 40B development off Howard Street and the Board provided
  20| its comments to the Town Manager.  The Planning Board in conjunction with many other Boards, Commissions, Town
  21| Officials, and private citizens submitted numerous comments outlining the Town’s opposition to the development of 200
  22| apartment units at 400 Howard St. The development of affordable housing is critical to Lunenburg; however, the site in
  23| question has significant flaws that concern Town Officials.  The applicant is currently going through the public hearing
  24| process with the Zoning Board of Appeals.
  25| The Board submitted four articles for November 2021 Special Town Meeting.
  26|    Two of the articles, which passed, updated the zoning bylaw to reflect the current zoning;
  27|    Two articles were submitted on behalf of the Water District to update the Watershed Protection Map.  DEP
  28|     requested additional changes to the proposed bylaw which Board members were not comfortable with; therefore, the
  29|     Board chose to pass over the Water District articles.
  30| The Land Use Director worked on a new draft for 2022 Annual Town Meeting.  The two Water District articles were
  31| submitted for May 2022 town meeting, but did not pass.
  32| A third article was also submitted for 2022 Annual Town Meeting which added clarification to the permitting process for
  33| non-medical marijuana establishments.  That article passed.
  34| Uma Flowers, a cannabis retail facility, was awarded a Community Host Agreement from Lunenburg and submitted an
  35| application for a state-licensed retail establishment under Section 250-4.15. Non-Medical Marijuana Establishments of
  36| the zoning bylaw.  The establishment will be located at 43 Summer Street (site of the former Donnelly’s Tavern).
  37| An application was received for construction of a 367,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building at 260
  38| Falulah Road.  The majority of the building was located on Airport Road/Crawford Street in Fitchburg, but a portion
  39|  extended into Lunenburg.
  40| Approval was given for the construction of a 372,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building off Leominster-
  41| Shirley Road near the Shirley town line.  As a result of this approval, improvements were made to Leominster-Shirley
  42| Road with the addition of an eastbound left-turn lane into the project and the widening of the road to the north (towards
  43| Reservoir Road) on the project side of the street.
  44| Under the Unitil Reliability Project, Unitil submitted an application for the removal and/or pruning of public shade trees
  45| along Leominster Road from Sunny Hill Road to Kilburn Street.  Some of the proposed trees already had contact with
  46| existing power lines.  Poles would be replaced and lines placed three to seven feet higher than the current placement.  By
  47| upgrading the existing lines Unitil expected to reduce outage time for incidents that occurred from the center of town to
  48| Goodrich Street.

   1|                                                         ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2| The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2022.   There were several applicants seeking
   3| special permits and special permits for dimensional variations.  The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and
   4| fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00PM on an “as needed” basis or ZOOM as an alternative.
   5| On August 18, 2022 a 40B Comprehensive Permit was filed for property located at 318, 319 & 400 Howard Street, for a
   6| project known as Pond View Commons owned by The Homes at Aro Estates, LLC.
   7| The proposal is to construct three multifamily residential buildings under the Massachusetts Comprehensive Permit
   8| Statute, Massachusetts General Law’s c.40B, ss.20-23.
   9| The applicant Pond View Commons, LLC seeks to create 200 apartment units within three multifamily residential
  10| buildings.
  11| The Zoning Board consists of the following members:  Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice-Chairman,
  12| Members:  Hans Wentrup, David Blatt, Patrick Callahan and Anthony Nicastro and Donald Gurney, Associate members.
  13| Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  14| The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  15| 1.  To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town’s Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  16| 2.  To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town’s Protective By-laws.
  17| 3.  To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  18|     amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from
  19|     the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other
  20|     Administrative Official.
  21| 4.  To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 40B.
  22|     The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under Chapter
  23|     40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.  These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning
  24|     ordinances.
  25| The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue,
  26| Lunenburg.  Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and
  27| Thursday, closed Friday.  I
  28| f you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421 during regular
  29| business hours.  Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at www.Lunenburgma.gov.

===PAGE 100=== (layout, 48 lines)
   1|                                                         Lunenburg Public Schools
   2|                                                         Superintendent’s Message
   3| I am pleased to present my annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools.  I want to express my gratitude for a
   4| collaborative approach toward projecting and planning for current and future needs with the Town Manager, Heather
   5| Lemieux.  As the leader of the district, I am fortunate to work with a School Committee that is truly dedicated to serving
   6| the best interests of our students and appreciative that the Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee work
   7| collaboratively with the School Committee and School Department to problem-solve in order to better meet the
   8| educational needs of the children of Lunenburg.
   9| The district has remained focused on our Strategy for Continuous Improvement, which is aligned with our Mission and
  10| Vision:
  11| The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for life                           -
  12| long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, and rewarding lives.
  13| The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of attending to
  14| the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered instruction for deeper
  15|  learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills necessary for continuous learning for all.
  16| The Strategy is a living document.  Each August the District Leadership Team reviews the Strategy, its objectives and
  17| action plans. The Strategy objectives for the next two to three years are:
  18|           Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student-centered and inclusive
  19|           by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential work for transforming
  20|           practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually learning and adapting practice to
  21|           meet the needs of all students.
  22|           Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous learning in
  23|           a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values.
  24|           Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication, in order to
  25|           increase the organization’s capacity to execute the district’s mission and vision.
  26|           Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are provided equal
  27|           access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with an appropriate level of
  28|           support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient, confident, and capable young adults
  29|           adequately prepared for future challenges.
  30| Over the last several years we have worked to put in place the necessary infrastructure to support the academic
  31| achievement of all students.  We have established curriculum leadership positions, instructional specialists/coaches,
  32| teacher curriculum leaders; piloted and purchased evidence-based math and literacy curricula for Kindergarten (K)
  33| through grade 5; implemented a standards-based benchmark assessment in math and literacy for grades K through 8;
  34| provided job-embedded professional development for teachers in best instructional practices and using assessment data
  35| to inform their instruction; and hired tutoring/intervention staff for additional academic supports during the school day as
  36| well as after school in order to close learning gaps. The Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator
  37| positions have been and continue to be integral to all of this work.
  38| In order for students to be ready and able to learn in the classroom, behavioral health needs must be addressed.  To that
  39| end, we have developed a multi-tiered system of added guidance counselors, social workers and adjustment counselors;
  40| piloted and adopted a districtwide social-emotional learning curriculum.  Students receive social-emotional supports in
  41| small group with counselors or in a one-on-one setting when student need dictates.  Additionally, we have continued to
  42| provide Care Solace, an online resource with live 24/7 concierge meant to assist families and individuals in finding local
  43| mental health support. Care Solace is a tool for school staff and families to connect with community-based mental
  44| healthcare resources and providers. The vast database of mental health resources includes vetted local therapists and
  45|  programs.
  46| All of these supports have been absolutely critical in order to respond to the increase in significant behavioral health
  47|  needs of our students.
  48|                                                                          100

===PAGE 101=== (layout, 50 lines)
   1|                                                         Superintendent’s Message
   2| The district has been working with intent to foster a safe and caring environment and inclusive culture in our schools and
   3| district.  Last year we administered the National School Climate Center survey for families, students, and staff.  We
   4| analyzed results and used that data to develop goals to improve our schools’ climate.  We will repeat that survey this
   5| spring to see if changes have had any impact.  As we look to build a more inclusive culture in our schools, we also look
   6| to diversify our staff to be more reflective of our community.  To that end, a district team participated in the Department
   7| of Elementary and Secondary Education’s Teacher Diversification Professional Learning Network and as a result, have
   8| been examining our recruitment and hiring practices.  The Lunenburg School Committee is also committed to this work
   9| and requested an equity audit for our district.  The audit commenced in July 2022 and the final report was made available
  10| to the Lunenburg community in February 2023 via the district website with a request for feedback.  The District
  11| Leadership will use this report to develop action plans which will be included in the district’s Strategy for Continuous
  12| Improvement.  Once these plans are developed, as is our annual practice, the administration will seek the Lunenburg
  13|  School Committee’s endorsement.  We look forward to engaging in this work with stakeholders.
  14| Another way the district is seeking to build a more inclusive culture is through strengthening the family-school
  15| partnership.  This year Luneburg Public Schools was one of four districts/schools selected to participate in the
  16| Department of Elementary and Secondary Education’s Family-School Partnership Initiative Program.  We will be
  17| expanding this work to involve stakeholders. This work will also result in the development of action plans, which will be
  18|  incorporated into the Strategy for Continuous Improvement.
  19| It is important to note that the physical environment of our schools is also important to the learning experience of our
  20| students.  Turkey Hill Elementary School is an aging facility which was not designed for elementary age students.  It was
  21| originally a middle school.  The building presents challenges and limitations for our teachers in terms of delivery of
  22| instruction in the modern era.  Electrical outlets are extremely limited in the classrooms and the lack of energy efficient
  23| windows creates a challenging learning environment with very high and very low outdoor temperatures.  The building
  24| has aging systems that cannot be easily, quickly, or inexpensively addressed.  With each passing year, the risk of system
  25| failures looms larger.  It is important for the school district administration, School Committee, and other boards and
  26| committees to continue to engage in discussion regarding the future of Turkey Hill Elementary School.
  27| All of these individual efforts and discussions regarding the future are designed to have a cumulative impact on student
  28| achievement and growth, and overall well-being in the post-COVID years and beyond.  I wish to acknowledge and thank
  29| our staff for their professionalism and flexibility as we have continued to make adjustments in order to provide the best
  30| possible learning environment and experience for our students; our parents and families for their continued support and
  31| partnership; and the Lunenburg School Committee, Town Manager, and local boards and committees for their support of
  32| the schools.  We remain focused on improving the ways in which we support our students academically, socially and
  33| emotionally in partnership with parents, extended families, and the greater community.  Through this work we are on a
  34| journey together. Collectively we have been and will continue to make a difference for our students.  I think Maya
  35| Angelou has captured our journey well when she said, “I have great respect for the past.  If you don’t know where
  36| you’ve come from, you don’t know where you’re going.  I have respect for the past, but I’m a person of the moment.
  37| I’m here, and I do my best to be completely centered at the place I’m at, then I go forward to the next place.”
  38| Annually I conclude my report with the announcement of the Superintendent                     ’s Award recipient.  The selection process is
  39| always difficult as we have many hard-working, high-performing, community service-oriented students.  I am pleased to
  40| announce Cassandra Cohen as the recipient of the 2023 Superintendent’s Award.  Cassie’s academic accomplishments
  41| include: Academic Excellence Award in Foreign Language, English, History, Mathematics, and Science.  She is also a
  42| Smith Book Award recipient.  Cassie has been a member of the Science Olympiad, Golf, and Indoor Track teams.  She is
  43| a member of the World Hunger Task Force and has served as the Class Treasurer.  She has also volunteered for the                                th
  44| Lunenburg Food Pantry, served as a peer tutor, and works as an intern in our district’s curriculum office.  Since the 8
  45| grade, Cassie has been an assistant teacher at the Joseph C. Foster Religious School.  When asked for a quote that best
  46|  describes her, she offered this Dolly Parton quote:          “Find out who you are and do it on purpose.            ”
  47| Respectfully Submitted,
  48| Kate Burnham, Ed.D.
  49| Superintendent

  15|                             Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology Department
  16| The Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology (IT) Department maintained a critical role in the expansion of
  17| the technology in our classrooms.  The Department worked with staff and students to redefine what classroom
  18| instruction looks and feels like for our students.
  19| The school year began with all students reporting back in session after the COVID restraints with each student having
  20| access to a device for daily instruction.  Here is a summary of the district device model:
  21|    Lunenburg Primary School – the building have enough iPads to assist with instruction and testing at the K-2 level
  22|    Turkey Hill Elementary School – each classroom is equipped with Chromebook carts
  23|    Lunenburg Middle High School – each student has access to a personal Chromebook, which they can bring home for
  24|      their individual studies
  25| In addition, Turkey Hill Elementary School purchased a Touchview interactive screen for every classroom.   This
  26|  equipment assists with the collaboration between teachers and students and has improved student participation.
  27| Reliance on computers in the classroom and in the households was prevalent when students were burdening the network
  28| system.  With the assistance from E-rate funding, the district purchased new equipment for the building server rooms,
  29| including routers, switches, wireless access points and licenses.
  30| Besides expanding educational needs, the IT Department maintained the typical response to maintain the district                         ’s core
  31| technology services.  Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and explore
  32| innovations in education technology.  The district is still working with a consultant to audit current practices,
  33| applications and models to measure any areas for growth, improvement and capacity.
  34|  Respectfully Submitted,
  35| Michael Cassidy
  36| Business Manager/HR Director
