   1|                                                     Special Services Department
   2|                                             (data taken from 2021-2022 school year)
   3| From the March 1, 2022 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics – District Summary), there are 211 students with
   4| disabilities receiving special education or related  services in Lunenburg (last year there were 251).  This represents
   5| 13.2% of our total student population of 1,593 students (it was 15.9% last year).  The state average from the 2021-22
   6| Selected Populations Report (https://profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/selectedpopulations.aspx) is 19.4%.  From the
   7| March 1, 2022 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment Statistics – District Summary), the total number of students
   8| receiving services outside the district was 8 (last year was 12).  Of the 8 students, 4 are placed in Collaborative
   9| Placements, 2 are in Day Placements and 2 are in Residential Placements.  Our Advanced Community Experience
  10| Program (ACE), which serves students ages 18-22 years old, had 3 students enrolled last year.  Additionally, from the
  11| March 1, 2022 report, 130 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg
  12| (last year was 146).
  13|          For the 2022 school year, due to the COVID-19 pandemic, most districts and schools (including Lunenburg) did
  14| not receive an accountability determination in 2022.  Participation rates in MCAS this year were as follows:  High Needs
  15| = 99%, Low Income = 99%, EL and Former EL = 100%, and Students w/disabilities = 99%. This data can be found at
  16| the DESE website:
  17| http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=01620000&orgtypecode=5&
  18| Other data collected throughout the year is also important to consider when looking at the whole Special Services
  19| Department.  DESE requires us to collect data for certain Indicators they are targeting each year.  For our Indicators, the
  20| following information is available below and also can be found at: http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx?
  21| orgcode=01620000&
  22| Indicator 1 – Graduation Rate. For the 2020-2021 school year, the state target for Students with IEPs was 76.33% (was
  23| 73.4% last year). Lunenburg’s rate was 55.6% (was 86.7% last year) and the overall state rate was 80.4% (was 74.7%
  24| last year) for Students with IEPs.
  25| Indicator 2 – Dropout Rate.  For the 2020-2021 school year, the state target for Students with IEPs was 13.97% (was
  26| 1.7% last year). Lunenburg rate for Students with IEPs was 0.0% (it was “Suppresed*” last year) and the overall state
  27| rate was 10.4% (was 2.7% last year).  *Special Education data are suppressed for enrollment counts fewer than 6.
  28| Indicator 3C – Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS. 2020-21 was a benchmark year so there is no state
  29| target for the Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS in English/Language Arts and in Mathematics.
  30| The 2021 district-level data on the performance of all students with IEPs on MCAS, including Next-Generation MCAS,
  31| Legacy MCAS and the MCAS-Alt, is available at:
  32|     http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/mcas/subgroups2.aspx?linkid=25&orgcode=01620000&fycode=2021&orgtypecode=5
  33|    http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/mcas/achievement_alt_level.aspx?linkid=116&orgcode=01620000&fycode=2021&orgtypecode=5
  34| Indicator 4 – Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs.  For Lunenburg, our rate was 0.0% (last
  35| year it was 0.4%).  The state rate was .4%.   Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant discrepancies with the
  36| Suspension/Expulsion  rates  by  race  or  ethnicity.    In  2021,  Lunenburg  was  not  found  to  have  any  significant
  37| discrepancies due to these factors.
  38| Indicator 5    - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6-21 with IEPs. For 2020-2021, the state’s rates are as
  39| follows:  for Full Inclusion of students = 66.2%, Partial Inclusion = 13.7%, Substantially Separate Placements = 13.5%,
  40| and for Separate Schools/Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 6.5%.  In Lunenburg, our percentages are as
  41| follows:  Full Inclusion = 70.0%, Partial = 9.2%, Substantially Separate = 14.6% and Separate schools, Residential, etc =
  42| 6.3%.
  43| Indicator 6 – Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs.  The 2020-2021 state rates showed the percent
  44| of students receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full Inclusion early childhood
  45| program was 52.3%, in a Partial Inclusion Program was 16.1%, a Substantially Separate Placement (special education
  46| class, separate school, or residential facility) was 19.4%, and students not attending an early childhood program but
  47| receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was 12.0%. In Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate
  48| was 20.8%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 20.8%, Substantially Separate rate was 37.5%, and Related Services was
  49| 20.8%.

===PAGE 105=== (layout, 54 lines)
   1| Indicator 7 – Early Childhood Outcomes.  Districts participate in Indicator 7 data collection as part of a 4 year cycle in
   2| which they report exit data on children for whom they have collected entry level data; exit may occur at any time during
   3| the subsequent three year period as the children leave the preschool program.  Lunenburg’s 2020-2021 cohort reporting
   4| year results are  suppressed for districts reporting 10 or fewer students.
   5| Indicator 8 – Parent Involvement.  This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special education who
   6| report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results for students with IEPs.
   7| For 2020-2021 the state target was 89%.  Lunenburg’s rate was 57.1% and the state’s rate was 60.1% (from information
   8| collected in the Spring of 2019).
   9| Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special Education
  10| Categories.  In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in either case.
  11| Indicator 11 – Initial Evaluation Timelines.   This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent to evaluate,
  12| who were evaluated within the State established timeline (2017-2018). Data is reported for all initial evaluations initiated
  13| in October, November, and December of the reporting year.                     Lunenburg was rated 90% in compliance and the state
  14| average for this Indicator was 100%.
  15| Indicator 12 – Early Childhood Transition.  This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C, found eligible
  16| for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third birthdays. The state target
  17| for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as               “Suppressed” (last school year reviewed was 2017-18). Data is
  18| suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer than
  19| 10.
  20| Indicator 13 – Secondary Transition.  In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%.  This indicator
  21| measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary
  22| goals that are annually updated and based upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including
  23| courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related
  24| to the student's transition services needs.  Lunenburg was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in
  25| compliance.
  26| Indicator 14 – Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs.  This indicator measures the % of students with IEPs who
  27| exited  high  school  during  the  2019-2020           school  year  and  self-reported  post-school  engagement  in  education  or
  28| employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
  29|    Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  30|    Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high school.
  31|    Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training program; or
  32|      competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high school.
  33| Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-secondary
  34| activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2020-2021 school year, state targets for the three measurements of
  35| Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A: 40.0%; Measurement B: 73.0%; and Measurement C: 79.0%.  Lunenburg
  36| rates  are  as  follows  (from  reporting  year  2020-2021):    Measurement  A  =  50%,  Measurement  B  =  75%,  and
  37| Measurement C = 75%.
  38| The Parent Advisory Council Supporting All Learners (PACSAL) continues to be an active support group for parents,
  39| teachers and administration.  All parents and community members can join the group, which currently consists of
  40| parents of students receiving Title I services and parents of students with disabilities.  Meetings are currently being held
  41| quarterly, 6pm, via Zoom.  The executive board is made up of a Chairperson: Nichole Camelo.
  42|          For more information, go to: https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/lps/department-directory/special-
  43| services/pacsal?authuser=0  In addition to the district PACSAL, Lunenburg also participates in a Regional SEPAC
  44| group.  Information from this group is on Facebook at “Regional SEPAC – Central MA”
  45| The Lunenburg Public Schools continues to provide a variety of special needs services to meet the varying needs of our
  46| students in the least restrictive environment. We have an experienced, hard working staff that works together with
  47| parents to provide these varied services.   Our parents are equal partners in our efforts to develop challenging and
  48| focused educational programs for children.  We remain committed to the Lunenburg Public Schools mission, “The
  49| mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for life-long
  50| learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, and rewarding lives.” This
  51| mission, we feel, was even able to be upheld during, and following, the pandemic.  Special education services were still
  52| able to be delivered to students and all DESE recommendations and protocols were put into place and followed in order
  53| to meet the needs of IEPs as best as possible.

  13|                                           Teaching & Learning Models and PD Update
  14| Lunenburg Public Schools returned to a full learning model for the 2021-2022 school year.  Students are invested in
  15| learning, and teachers are working diligently to evaluate student progress, identify learning gaps, make adjustments to
  16| practice, and provide scaffolds accordingly.  We have implemented structured common planning time for teachers in
  17| grade level content areas so that teachers may unpack and plan for units of study, align to standards, and look at data and
  18| student work.  Our district literacy specialist and our math instructional specialist meet with teachers during common
  19| planning time to support curriculum development and help foster student growth.  High school department heads
  20| structure department meeting time to facilitate the necessary curriculum work needed to move each department forward
  21| and improve learning for students.
  22| Students in grades K     -8 completed the fall and winter iReady diagnostic assessment in both reading and math, and
  23| teachers are participating in data cycles to analyze data, plan for targeted support, and adjust instruction. Personalized
  24| learning platforms through iReady provide differentiated instruction, systematic practice, and scaffolded feedback in
  25| both reading and math.
  26| The Lunenburg Public School District is participating in the Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Literacy
  27| Academy and Social Emotional Learning Academy through the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education
  28| (DESE).  The Literacy Academy provides coaching sessions and technical assistance in specific grade levels for literacy,
  29| including a focus on understanding the science of reading and standards-based lesson planning.  Both Academies assist
  30| our district with creating multi-tiered systems of support with a focus on tier I, II, and III in academics and social
  31| emotional learning and behavioral health.
  32| A K-5 math council including grade level teachers, special services teachers, EL teachers, and administrators, has been
  33| working with DESE’s Evaluating and Selecting High-Quality Materials Network to review and select a core math
  34| curriculum.  We are planning to begin a field test and pilot of several programs beginning in February and make a final
  35| decision regarding purchase this spring.
  36| Math teachers in grades K-5 have been working with a math consultant and have garnered new strategies for
  37| mathematical discourse routines.  Twenty staff members from throughout the district, including guidance counselors,
  38| social workers, and teachers were trained and have been implementing the Trails to Wellness curriculum, which is an
  39| evidence based social emotional wellness program rooted in cognitive behavioral ideology.
  40| Our Professional Development Committee continues to check-in with the district to evaluate needs and prepare for
  41| upcoming professional learning including future book studies and workshops.  Our administrative team and our
  42| curriculum leaders are working with a consultant on workshops pertaining to diversity, equity, and inclusion and plan to
  43| proceed with this work to include the entire district staff.  The team is also participating in a book study based on the
  44| book Caste, by Isabel Wilkerson, which takes a close look at social constructs in the United States.   Our team is
  45| studying these constructs in connection with our school system and identifying ways we can improve student inclusion
  46| and experiences in Lunenburg.
  47| We are pleased to have introduced a new After School Program at the Lunenburg Primary School, Turkey Hill
  48| Elementary School, Lunenburg Middle School and Lunenburg High School.  The program for grades 1-5 provides
  49| targeted support in literacy and math in addition to a Friday enrichment day.  The program for grades 6-12 provides
  50| academic support for students’ current classes in addition to support with executive functioning skills.  Our first session
  51| will be running for seven weeks followed by a second session for an additional ten weeks.
  52|                                                                         106

  17| The Massachusetts legislature has provided funding to make meals available at no cost to all students during school year
  18| 2022-2023, regardless of household income, ensuring all students have access to the nutrition they need during the
  19| school day. Unfortunately, the waiver allowing school food service programs to serve non-congregate meals ended
  20| forcing districts to provide seat time for served meals. As a result, Lunenburg school food service had to stop serving
  21| grab and go lunches on early release days. In an effort to ensure students continue to benefit from school meals on
  22| early release days, we will enhance our breakfast options on these days. Our program like many others in United States
  23| continues to struggle with the supply chain breakdown and price increases.
  24| Lunenburg School Lunch Program is participating in Harvest of the Month again this year.                        Massachusetts Harvest of
  25| the Month program promotes a different Massachusetts-produced food each month in cafeterias and meal sites across
  26| the state. Harvest of the Month has provided our program the opportunity to celebrate local foods, experiment with
  27| new recipes and lead the way in making farm-fresh foods available to students and staff all while supporting local
  28|  farmers.

===PAGE 108=== (layout, 46 lines)
   1|                                              LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2| The Lunenburg Primary School is a student focused, early childhood, learning community serving students enrolled in
   3| preschool through grade 2. Student ages range from three to eight years of age. Our community is composed of
   4| students, families, instructional and support staff. Our focus is on the academic, social emotional growth, and
   5| development of the whole child.
   6| The October 1, 2023 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education was 387
   7| students. With 44 preschool students, 106 kindergarten students, 125 first grade students and 112 second grade
   8| students. Class sizes range in 5 kindergarten classes from 19-23 students, from 18-22 students in six grade one classes,
   9| and from 20-23 in  five grade two classes.
  10| Our professional development has centered on implementing two new evidenced based curriculums in math and
  11| literacy from HMH. Into Reading and Into Math. Common Planning Time continues to be a priority for our building
  12| schedule and the re-allocation of existing support staff to address student needs based on student performance data.
  13| We have been able to access consultants as well district curriculum coordinators so that teachers have an
  14| opportunity as a whole grade level to develop and provide evidence based instruction, common learning
  15| experiences, student data analysis and share effective teaching strategies to support student academic
  16| growth.
  17| Our staff works to maintain a school culture, which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. We continue to implement
  18| the Responsive Classroom model in all of our classrooms.  Positive Behavioral Interventions and Supports (PBIS) is
  19| an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and integrating all of the data, systems, and practices
  20| affecting student outcomes every day.  We continue to use Trails to Wellness, which is a whole-school/district-wide
  21| approach to teaching social emotional learning. It is designed to help students recognize, understand,
  22| describe, communicate and regulate their emotions and provide them with the tools they need to create and
  23| maintain positive social interaction with their peers.
  24| The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to ensure that all
  25| students have the services they need to be successful.  Common assessments are completed in the fall, winter, and
  26| spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These common assessments serve to
  27| monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the progress of students. Struggling students are identified based
  28| on assessment data for access to our current Multi-Tiered Support System (MTSS) in Reading, Math, Speech,
  29| Occupational and Physical Therapy. The MTSS model is also used to provide social emotional support for students
  30| that are referred to our building Behavioral Health Support Team.
  31| The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns.  Serving as an advisory group to
  32| the principal, the council is charged with            reviewing the school building budget and developing the school
  33| improvement plan.
  34| Members of the Council for the 2022-2023 school year are:
  35| Principal:                                                            Chad Adams
  36| Assistant Principal:                                                  Gina Kutarnia
  37| Teachers:                                                    Kirsten Snape, Kara Slattery, Serene Pease, Melanie Roy, Tara Bastien
  38| Parents:                                                  Meaghan Arnold, Shaunna Baril, Ashley Steele
  39| School Committee Rep:                                     Laura Kelly
  40| We are truly grateful for the opportunity to work with our highly dedicated district wide colleagues and a caring
  41| community in order to provide the best possible educational opportunities for our youngest students.
  42| For more information about the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website:  https://
  43| sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/primary/
  44| Respectfully submitted,
  45| Chad Adams, Principal

===PAGE 110=== (layout, 51 lines)
   1|                                                     Turkey Hill Elementary School
   2| It has been a remarkable year of recovery and growth for our school community.  A full two years after the COVID
   3| epidemic so profoundly affected all of our personal and professional lives; we finally had the luxury to implement
   4| “normal” protocols again in our classrooms.  The masks became voluntary, social distancing officially ended, Plexiglas
   5| shields were stored away, and children were allowed to work and play with their peers without restrictions.  In March,
   6| we unrolled our classroom carpets, grouped desks together for collaborative learning, and many other restrictions were
   7| eliminated.  We invited parents back into our building with both a Memorial Day celebration in May and moving up rd
   8| ceremony for fifth graders in June.  All students enjoyed exciting new experiences on field trips:  3thth                    graders to the
   9| Ecotarium, 4      graders to Leominster State Forest, and 5            graders to Polar Park in Worcester for a WooSox game. It was
  10| wonderful to operate as a fully functioning, fully present elementary school again.
  11| In the fall of 2022 Turkey Hill Elementary School had 358 enrolled students. There are five sections of grade 3 and
  12| grade 5, with a total of 111 students and 105 students respectively.  There are six sections of grades 4 with 142 students.
  13| We employ 27 teachers, 100% of whom are licensed in their assignment area. Our special areas include physical
  14| education, art, music, library, STEM, and social emotional learning. We are also served by a full time assistant principal,
  15| two full time nurses, a psychologist, BCBA, a full time adjustment counselor/social worker and a guidance counselor.
  16| Other support personnel who work across the district include a literacy specialist/Title 1 coordinator, a math specialist,
  17| the Director of Teaching and Learning, two EL teachers, a speech and language pathologist, OT, and PT. We continue to
  18| benefit from the services of an interventionist and two supporting tutors. They work under the direction of our literacy
  19| specialist and provide targeted support for small groups of students.  Our talented front office administrative assistant,
  20| Ms. McAllister, effectively keeps all of us organized, ensuring that students’ after school and dismissal plans are
  21| conveyed to teachers, welcoming new families and helping them through registration, and keeping administrators on
  22| track each day.
  23| Staff rededicated themselves to our mission of providing all students with the skills, confidence and passion for lifelong
  24| learning, and students focused to develop their skills in literacy and math, social studies and science, as well as the
  25| special area subjects. Beyond that, our school enjoyed several enriching events.
  26| In January, May and September, all students participated in the iReady reading and math benchmark assessments, and
  27| we celebrated students’ academic growth and effort.  We continue to address the negative impact of remote learning,
  28| and data provided by these assessments helps refine our focus for interventions.
  29| In March and April, THES and Primary School staff collaborated under the guidance of curriculum director Annica
  30| Scott and literacy coach/Title 1 coordinator Deidre Mclean to review, trial and finally select research based, standards
  31| aligned curriculum for both math and science.
  32| They chose Houghton Mifflin Harcourt’s Into Reading and Into Math, and over the summer many teachers started to
  33| familiarize themselves with the expansive elements of the programs.
  34| Our annual spelling bee was held in February, and Miss Chloe McKenna withstood the challenges of unfamiliar words
  35| and several fierce competitors to prevail as our school spelling champion.
  36| In March, 4th grade parents and students were introduced to a new iteration of Nature’s Classroom by Mrs. Rhonda
  37| Connery, in preparation for a fall week at Grotonwood.  Every 5th student except a few away on vacation participated in
  38| the Nature’s Classroom during the last week of September, with the additional option of attending only the day program
  39| or staying overnight.  There were some tears shed on the Monday bus ride to Groton waving good-bye to parents and a
  40| few more tears fell on the Friday bus ride home, departing from their camp counselors, as new bonds of friendship were
  41| formed.  It was a successful week with good weather, community building, and the creation of fond memories of a
  42| unique time together, thanks to the combined efforts of our entire fifth grade staff.
  43| We held a fully in-person open house in early September (the first in three years). It was a well-attended event and for
  44| many parents their first opportunity to be physically present in our building.  It was accompanied by a book fair
  45| facilitated by our new librarian, Mrs. Mack. Parent conferences in October offered both in person and virtual options and
  46| appointments were fully booked within a few days.
  47| Other highlights from the fall season included the Girls on The Run program, a research based program designed to help
  48| girls understand themselves better, value their relationships and teamwork, and recognize how they can shape the world
  49| to be a better place! Over 30 girls participated, with the assistance of many parent and high school volunteers, headed by
  50| Coach Nichole Camelo.
  51|                                                                         110

===PAGE 111=== (layout, 32 lines)
   1| We held our annual Penny Wars fundraiser to help purchase exciting new technology for our classrooms. As always, our
   2| PTO parents and volunteers assisted in the weeklong collection, headed by parent Alex Lind; we could not be successful
   3| without their efforts .
   4| All sixteen homerooms as well as our five special education rooms were outfitted with TouchView Panels. These 65
   5| inch interactive displays allow teachers to create and demonstrate any content to the class from their computers
   6| Text is easier to read. Students can manipulate the on-screen content as a team and problem solve.  These have become
   7| essential tools for the classrooms displaying the new literacy and math curricula digital content.
   8| We were able to bring back a fifth grade band and chorus program under the direction of our new music teacher, Mrs.
   9| Alice Daugherty.  The chorus delighted our veteran guests at the Veteran                   ’s Day ceremony with their performance of an
  10| Armed Forces medley of the United States. There were 39 veteran guests, relatives of our students, who honored us with th
  11| their presence on November 10            .
  12| Our assistant principal, Mr. John Palumbo, has led the staff in the effective implementation of both PBIS and restorative
  13| justice at Turkey Hill.  Positive Behavioral Intervention Supports (PBIS) involves using evidence                         -based prevention and
  14| intervention practices to support academic, social, emotional and behavioral competence of all students.  THES has
  15| teamed with the Primary School to provide similar protocols and language about behavior expectations across all school
  16| areas, which are modeled and agreed upon with visual reminders throughout the school.
  17| Restorative justice focuses on mediation and agreement rather than punishment. It                      ’s about accepting responsibility for
  18| harm and making restitution. Our focus has shifted from a penalty to                  “how can we make it right?         ”  Students have been
  19| remarkably engaged in offering apologies, cleaning up, and making appropriate amends. Staff also use incentive
  20| programs for struggling students to help manage challenging behaviors and develop their self                         -confidence and self-
  21| esteem.
  22| From September to December, 2021 there were 83 discipline referrals and 7 suspensions.  From September to December,
  23| 2022 there were 53 discipline referrals and 4 suspensions.  We attribute this impressive 36% decrease in referrals to the
  24| efforts of the staff to implement PBIS over the last two years. We have also noticed fewer repeat offenders as restorative
  25| justice is teaching students about accepting responsibility for their behavior.
  26| Our school’s success relies upon the combined and collaborative efforts of our wonderful staff, parents, and community
  27| members.  We continue to seek out opportunities to engage and effectively communicate with our families.  Turkey Hill
  28| may be a well-worn building, constructed in 1969, but it is also a well                -loved and cared for community of students
  29| supported by parents, staff, and Lunenburg residents.  I am honored to serve as principal.
  30| Respectfully Submitted,
  31| Heidi Champagne, Principal

===PAGE 113=== (layout, 45 lines)
   1|                                                           Lunenburg Middle School
   2| Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support students                                      ’
   3| social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated, creative, lifelong learner
   4| in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest communication.   The dedicated and
   5| accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of educating the whole child
   6| academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by which all students become positive community
   7| members that possess the skills to be successful in high school and beyond.
   8| Effective, student centered middle schools embrace interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable teachers to work with  Lunenburg Middle School is in its sixth year as a co-located middle - high school serving grades six through eight.
   9| smaller groups of students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn and engage students in their preferred
  10| learning style, making them more confident and successful.
  11| Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of different
  12| interests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities. One of our goals is for
  13| students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue exploring as they move through high
  14| school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that foster critical thinking and problem
  15| solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond or where their passion may lead them.
  16|                                                                      August 2022
  17| With the return to a sense of normalcy at the beginning of the school year, we are fortunate to not have any mandated
  18| health or safety protocols associated with the global pandemic. We have identified that a large number of students
  19| suffered some type of “learning loss” during the course of the pandemic. This means students may not be functioning at
  20| grade level in the areas of ELA and Math.
  21| In order to combat this learning loss, LMS is utilizing the iReady  learning platform to provide an additional learning
  22| component in addition to classroom instruction.  iReady is being implemented during our intervention blocks and
  23| provides teachers with individualized learning paths based upon the students individual learning needs.  Parents will be
  24| provided fall, winter and spring benchmark assessments created by iReady so they too can monitor student progress.
  25| Lunenburg Middle School has benefited from a top-notch guidance support team that we have had in place since 2021.
  26| Two guidance counselors, a school social worker coupled with our shared school psychologist has been able to provide a
  27| level of social emotional support to our students and their families that we have not been accustomed to. Data supports
  28| the need for these positions as during the pandemic, the number of student’s experiencing anxiety and depression has
  29| significantly increased nationwide and in our own community. Our guidance team has been able to meet the needs of our
  30| students while preparing them to be available for academic learning.
  31| Our grade eight team of teachers continue to implement a schedule that mirrors the high school schedule. Our
  32| philosophy is to ease the difficult transition from middle school to the first year of high school by developing student’s
  33| independent skills they will require to be successful in high school. Grade eight teachers function as one inclusive team
  34| where students take classes with a variety of different teachers as they would in a high school setting. This differs from
  35| our two interdisciplinary team of teachers that have been a successful model in grades six and seven.

   3| category and then the column next to it shows our scores in comparison with the state scores in schools who service
   4| similar grade levels. As you can see, even with the effects of the pandemic, LMS scores are above the state percentage
   5| in six out of the seven areas tested. In grade 8 Science Technology and Engineering our scores are over 20 points
   6| higher than the state average, an impressive number. We look forward to what 2023 scores will indicate after the
   7| specific interventions we have put in place for students to be successful.
   8| Athletics and clubs
   9| LMS successfully participated in middle school Field Hockey and Boys and Girls Cross Country and this winter we
  10| will continue with middle school basketball after many years of it being eliminated. The opportunities middle school
  11| sports provides to students to participate, learn and become part of an effective team atmosphere is immeasurable.
  12| Science Olympiad, Drama Club, Yearbook Club and middle school Student Government return as favorite activities
  13| for students to engage in. Student Government continues to provide opportunities for students to engage in age
  14| appropriate extracurricular activities like dances, spirit weeks and movie nights.
  15| Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that ensure student
  16| success and promote a passion for lifelong learning. The key components to our success continue to be our talented
  17| faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and
  18| learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning. Together we continue to
  19| make the difference in the lives of students.

===PAGE 115=== (layout, 41 lines)
   1|                                                         Lunenburg High School
   2| Lunenburg High School and its faculty continue to focus on rigorous curriculum, high standards and opportunities for
   3| students, both academic and extracurricular, so that every student feels supported and included in our community. LHS
   4| faculty strives to reflect on their teaching practices, to learn and to change in order to best serve all of our students.
   5|                                                            2022-2023 School year
   6| In August of 2022, Lunenburg High School was able to return to school without health and safety protocols due to the
   7| pandemic for the first time in two and a half years. Our entire school community did an outstanding job of educating
   8| students through the pandemic, our efforts now focus on identifying students who were struggling and providing
   9| educational services that would help them succeed in their high school careers and beyond.
  10| One of the components of that support was the addition of a LHS Intervention Teacher. This teaching position was
  11| created in order to provide direct instruction to students who were identified by their teachers as to needing additional
  12| support to be successful in the classroom. Students report to the Interventionist during their study skills block and
  13| receive assistance to the specific class or classes they are struggling in. The initial data collected as to the effectiveness
  14| of this position and the direct impact on students classroom grades and learning are remarkable.
  15| LHS also adopted an additional Algebra Essentials II class that was developed to ensure specific students were able to
  16| access the curriculum and state standards. Creating this additional class benefited all students, as it was able to reduce
  17| class sizes in Algebra II and allow teachers to provide more personalized, individualized instruction while working with
  18| smaller groups of students in a traditionally academically demanding course.
  19| The addition to the faculty and courses at Lunenburg High School have allowed students a more varied options for
  20| course selection, provided individualized and programmatic support and structure for students to be successful. LHS
  21| looks forward to reporting out on the difference in student learning these positions have made.
  22|                                                              MCAS RESULTS
  23|                                  Lunenburg High School - Spring 2022 MCAS Results (Grade 10)

  25|                                                           Expectations

  28| The results indicate the LHS percentage scores for meeting and exceeding expectations are high, over 10 percentage
  29| points higher than the state average when compared to schools who serve students in similar grade levels.
  30| We were also very pleased to learn that 29 students at Lunenburg High School received the state’s John and Abigail
  31| Adams Scholarship this year.  The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance puts them at the top 25
  32| percent of their district.  To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other, or
  33| Advanced on both the English and Math exams.

===PAGE 121=== (layout, 39 lines)
   1|                                   The 2021-2022 school year was my eighth year serving as School Principal at Monty Tech,
   2|                                   and  I  am  honored  that  it  concluded  with  my  appointment  to  a  new  role                   –   that  of
   3|                                   Superintendent-Director.  Having been part of this remarkable  educational community,
   4|                                   developing a deep understanding of initiatives – both large and small – that are an integral
   5|                                   part of our educational programs, and working with and learning from our outstanding
   6|                                   faculty, staff and administrative team, has allowed for a successful transition into this new
   7|                                   position.  I could not be more grateful for the encouraging and kind remarks I continue to
   8|                                   receive from students and parents, faculty and staff, and the regional business community,
   9|                                   whose support means so much to the Monty Tech family.
  10|                                   With the effects of the pandemic still being felt, students and staff were encouraged to
  11|                                   maintain health and safety standards learned in the previous year, but to also return to a new
  12| “normal.”  Daily in-person instruction, off-campus construction projects, and Skills competitions returned.  While the
  13| pandemic certainly brought questions, fears, and insecurities to the surface for many, it also provided instructional staff
  14| with opportunities to collaborate  and develop new skills in infusing technology into daily lessons.  Monty Tech
  15| instructors worked tirelessly, with a new appreciation for instructional technology, to embed some of “what worked” into
  16| their daily, class instruction.  Collecting assignments via Google Classrooms, conducting quick learning assessments via
  17| apps like Quizizz or Kahoot, and assigning group learning projects using Google Slides and Docs, teachers resulted in a
  18| heightened level of student engagement.
  19| The regional workforce also seemed to find its footing in FY22, welcoming approximately 200 students into local shops
  20| and businesses to work and learn alongside industry professionals, through the school’s long-standing co-op program.
  21| Another remarkable example of school-to-business collaboration was demonstrated when eight businesses submitted
  22| letters of support to Commonwealth Corporation on the school’s behalf.  Attesting to their belief in Monty Tech training
  23| programs, agreeing to interview program completers, and even highlighting Monty Tech graduates currently on staff,
  24| these letters were instrumental in the acquisition of a $600,000 grant award, which would provide no-cost training for
  25| young adults throughout our area.  Monty Tech understands the value of a vocation, and through the Career Technical
  26| Initiative, we have been able to extend training opportunities beyond the school day, helping a wide range of adults train
  27| for those high wage, high skills jobs that remained unfilled when the pandemic subsided.
  28| As the value of a vocational education becomes more evident, we have seen applications to attend Monty Tech on the
  29| rise.  While we understand that frustration that can come with not being admitted to a school of your choosing, we are
  30| challenging ourselves to work with local school leaders to establish practices and programs that will ensure as many
  31| students as possible are admitted from each sending community, and that all students who are interested in the school
  32| have an opportunity to visit and tour our campus.  Undoubtedly, a highlight of the 2021-2022 school year was seeing all
  33| eighth grade students from Fitchburg Public Schools join our tour day event.   I look forward to further discussions and
  34| collaborations with area school leaders, so that we may establish innovative training programs that will serve those who
  35| want a vocational experience, but have not yet attended Monty Tech.
  36| I remain humbled and honored to lead such an outstanding organization, and thank you for your continued support.
  37| Respectfully submitted,
  38| Thomas R. Browne,         Superintendent-Director
  39|                                                                        121

   2| School Committee, administration, and staff.              The final fiscal year 2021-2022 Educational Plan totaled $28,605,425
   3| which represents a 0.73% decrease over the 2020-2021 Educational Plan.  The District’s FY22 budget only exceeds the
   4| minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by $203,320 or 0.78%.

===PAGE 124=== (layout, 32 lines)
   1| As vocational schools across the Commonwealth grow in popularity, and the value of vocational training becomes more
   2| evident, we have seen a steady increase in the
   3| number of students applying for admission to
   4| Monty Tech.
   5| With  seats  available  limited  only  by  our
   6| building capacity, and a deep understanding
   7| of  the  increasing  demand,  school  leaders
   8| continue   to   work   with   area   school   and
   9| business   leaders   to   develop   and   expand
  10| programs to address this concern.  Whether
  11| these   new   programs   are   offered   in   the
  12| evening  through  the  School  of  Continuing
  13| Education,  or  even  during  the  traditional
  14| school day, our goal is to expand access so
  15| that students who hope to enter the workforce
  16| may do so with a set of valuable skills and
  17| industry-recognized    credentials    that    will
  18| make them more employable, and better equipped to grow and learn in the occupation of their choosing.
  19| While Monty Tech continues to ensure that students are job ready immediately after graduation, not all students who
  20| graduate from Monty Tech choose to enter the workforce upon completion.  In fact, several of the school                             ’s vocational
  21| training programs prepare students for advanced education and training in high-wage, high-skill careers in engineering,
  22| allied health, business, education and computer sciences that demand a bachelor’s and even master’s degree.  As such, it
  23| is imperative that the school also prepare those students for college level coursework.
  24| Students who attend Monty Tech must successfully complete all of the same academic requirements that students in
  25| comprehensive high schools complete, but at Monty Tech, students are only in academic classes every other week.
  26| Many students may find adjusting to this week on                 – week off schedule challenging, but it is this same schedule that
  27| provides Monty Tech students with time to explore and learn in a chosen vocational program at a deeper level.  Whether
  28| running  the  school’s  on-site,  full-service  restaurant,  operating  the  School  Store,  building  a  home  for  Habitat  for
  29| Humanity, or performing community services across our sending communities, Monty Tech students are developing
  30| technical skills, while also learning critical time management and study skills.  We are confident that whether our
  31| graduates choose to pursue college or career pathways, they are prepared for what lies ahead.
  32|                                                                         124

===PAGE 125=== (layout, 36 lines)
   1| During the spring of 2022, 10th grade students took the Next Generation MCAS examination in English Language Arts,
   2| Mathematics, and Biology.  In addition, select 9th graders also sat for the Next Generation MCAS Biology exam.
   3| Students performed well across all subject areas, with a passing rate of 100% in English Language Arts, 97% in
   4| Mathematics, and 97% in Biology.
   5| While Monty Tech is certainly known for its strong vocational training programs, its academic programs continue to
   6| evolve  to  prepare  graduates  for  diverse  and  challenging  college  and  career  pathways,  and  are  widely  known  to
   7| effectively prepare students at all levels to reach their goals.  A focus in recent years has been                the incorporation of new
   8| and updated materials and coursework to expand STEM opportunities for students.  In FY22, Monty Tech applied for a
   9| Massachusetts Life Science Center grant, developed in collaboration with our post-secondary partners at Fitchburg State
  10| University.  Using the $110,000 award, Monty Tech instructors will work collaboratively to align course expectations
  11| with FSU’s Anatomy & Physiology class, laying a foundation for a first-ever articulation agreement with FSU.
  12| Instructors look forward to participating in curriculum development activities at the University, and will attend related,
  13| immersive professional development programs to build capacity and collect/share valuable expertise from colleagues.
  14| Additionally, when the proposed agreement (Dual Enrollment or Articulation) is finalized, students who successfully
  15| complete the course will earn 4 college credits, saving them time and as much as $1,900 as they consider pursuing
  16| STEM college pathways.
  17| Monty Tech students also benefitted from the establishment of another new science course in FY22, Microbiology.
  18| Instructors  developed the new curriculum and  travelled to Texas to participate  in the  National Science  Teaching
  19| Association Annual Conference, while school officials renovated a science lab to replicate a biomedical facility.  All new
  20| course materials, equipment and technology was purchased and the addition of a valuable STEM opportunity has been a
  21| welcome addition to an already rigorous science program.
  22| In May of 2022, 120 students participated in AP Exams. Those 120 students took 162 AP Exams collectively. An
  23| impressive 82 of the 120 students were eligible to receive college credit for qualifying scores of 3+. That is a 68.33%
  24| pass rate, which is higher than the global average and a significant increase from the previous school year.
  25| Additionally, to support the expansion of AP course offerings, three instructors completed the training to teach AP
  26| Seminar during the summer of 2022. AP Seminar is now offered as a co-taught elective course for juniors. As an
  27| extension of AP Seminar, instructors will be trained to teach AP Research during the summer of 2023. Together, AP
  28| Seminar and AP Capstone will allow MT students to qualify for the AP Capstone designation upon graduation. Students
  29| who earn the AP Capstone designation are highly qualified for college success, and thus, more likely to gain admission
  30| into competitive colleges and universities.
  31|                                                             2018              2019              2020               2021              2022
  32|                              Total AP Students                95               113               106               142               120
  33|                              Number of Exams                 134               148               146               189               162
  34|                 AP Students with Scores 3+                    46                69                75                61                82
  35|   % of Total AP Students with Scores 3+                    48.42%            61.06%            70.75%            42.96%            68.33%

   3| learning.

   5| important work of building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, making walkways

   7| students once again demonstrated their talents on live clientele.  The 2021-2022 school year was a busy one for our
   8| vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide practical, hands-on learning experiences for our students, while
   9| helping to improve each of the eighteen member communities.

  15| networking and information technology.            In 2021-2022, all Freshmen completed

  17| -out PC Pro Certificate exam, the Cisco IT Essential course for computer repair

  20| Programming curriculum.          Four seniors qualified for the AP Computer Science

  25| semifinals. Two Seniors and two Juniors were on co-op placements during the
  26| year, applying their technical skills in area businesses.  In total, approximately 100
  27| hours of technical support were completed at the program’s Help Desk, where students worked on hardware and
  28| software troubleshooting on desktops, laptops, printers, and mobile devices. (Total student enrollment: 41)
  29| Masonry:  The talents of Monty Tech Masonry students are on display throughout
  30| district communities, as numerous projects that were completed during the 2021-
  31| 2022 school year, including constructing scaffolding at the Habitat for Humanity

  35| sidewalks and stairs to ensure a safe environment for students, staff and guests.

  39| industry experts.  The school’s co-op program is an important example of school-

  27| Welding Code Qualification Test. (Total student enrollment: 59)
  28| The Monty Tech Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student’s technical education that combines
  29| classroom instruction with on-the-job-training.  The popular Co-op Program provides students with an opportunity to
  30| further develop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry-related work environment. All students are
  31| eligible to participate in the Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and school grade, attendance and performance
  32| requirements, and all Co-op students have completed the OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course
  33| prior to being placed in a work environment.
  34| The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer.  For the student, it is a chance to gain
  35| on-the-job training that improves both their technical    proficiency and employability skills,    and    making    industry
  36| connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities.  The Co-op Program also allows students to earn while
  37| they learn, which has allowed many students to learn valuable life skills, financial literacy skills, and gain a deeper
  38| understanding of the importance of career advancement and self-advocacy.
  39| Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner. Co-op students provide a pool of trained talent,
  40| ready to assume temporary, part-time or even full-time opportunities, thus reducing employer training costs.   Co-op work hours may
  41| also be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers.
  42| In 2021 -2022, the school saw a return to the workplace, and significant increases in the number of students placed in this
  43| valuable training program.  With approximately 100 area employers participating in Cooperative Education, providing
  44| valuable experience to almost 200 students by the end of the 2021-2022 school year, school officials remain grateful for
  45| their continued support, and look forward to future collaborations.

   1| Given the exciting news that schools could return to                ‘normal’     during the 2021-2022 school year, the Department of
   2| Student Support Services focused its efforts on students’                 social and emotional wellness. Defining a new normal and
   3| providing structured supports to students as they acclimated to the demands, pace and rigor of academics, vocational
   4| programming and extracurricular activities was a top priority.   Staff reflected on student data, strengthened community
   5| partnerships, implemented new initiatives and expanded on the level of tiered supports offered to our student population.
   6| The department continued its important mission to break the stigma of mental health, knowing that everyone adjusts
   7| differently and handles stress in various ways. This unified approach, support, and collaboration helped our student body
   8| display resilience, grow individually, and meet success.
   9| At Monty Tech, we understand that everyone will require help at some point; that as individuals we all have strengths
  10| and weaknesses, and as a result, we strive to implement thoughtful tiered services that promote a feeling of comfort for
  11| students when accessing this support.  In addition to the department                 ’s current school    -based counseling services, Monty
  12| Tech and LUK, Inc. collaborated throughout 2021               -2022 with the Department of Public Health to provide services to at                  -
  13| risk students and their families.        The district also partnered with LUK to create a direct pipeline for telehealth referrals
  14| and increased access to counseling supports.               Programs such as the HOPE Squad; a peer to peer suicide prevention
  15| program, Project AMP; a program to address prevention of substance use among youth ages 13                            -17, and Students Taking
  16| A New Direction (STAND); a program to support students displaying at                     -risk behaviors have all been implemented.
  17| The Department of Student Support Services has taken the necessary measures to ensure Social Emotional Learning
  18| (SEL) is in the forefront of conversations when supporting students as they acclimate to returning to school full time
  19| after lengthy periods of time away.  Courses and activities designed to promote mindfulness, understanding, and social
  20| emotional wellness are part of the many course offerings.                Yoga and Meditative Art, Visual Arts, Directed Studies with
  21| Social Emotional Learning lessons are all part of the efforts being implemented to ensure we address the needs of all
  22| students and support their individual growth and development in a post pandemic world.
  23| During the 2021-2022 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided specialized
  24| services to approximately three hundred students                –  measuring progress of over two hundred students on Individual
  25| Education Plans (IEPs) and approximately one hundred students adhering to individualized Section 504 plans.  While the
  26| Student Support Services Department encompasses special education, the department provides support and is available
  27| to all Monty Tech students.

===PAGE 132=== (layout, 45 lines)
   1| The  Student  Support  Services  department  includes a  full-time  nursing  staff that continues to respond to  COVID
   2| concerns, administers medications, performs state-mandated health screening exams, and provides, when necessary,
   3| health information to the special education team for a student’s IEP meetings.  The department benefits from a full-time
   4| school social worker that participates in departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances,
   5| family issues, homelessness, maternity, health issues, proper food and clothing, and military status deployments.  The
   6| school is also fortunate to have on staff a full-time psychologist, who evaluates all students referred for an initial
   7| evaluation or who may require a three-year re-evaluation.  In addition, we have a full-time speech language pathologist,
   8| available to assist students with disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers.  Finally, our students also
   9| have  access  to  the  services  of  a  full-time  adjustment  counselor  and  part-time  school  psychologist.                     All  of  these
  10| individuals are available for scheduled counseling sessions, mental health emergency treatment, medical/mental health re
  11| -entry and transition support services, as well as crisis intervention.
  12| The school’s Director of Student Support Services oversees the District’s Special Education Program, which is reviewed
  13| annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements.  The comprehensive review and evaluation are done in
  14| collaboration with the Special Education Parent Advisory Council (SEPAC), and the results are used to improve the
  15| special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School.  The district
  16| will be working with the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) during the 2022-2023 to review
  17| all special education services, timelines, and programs.
  18| The 2021-2022 school year was a welcome return to normalcy at Monty Tech, relieving the Technology Department of
  19| the challenges presented by hybrid and remote instruction and learning that was necessary in previous years.  That also
  20| meant the department could focus its attention on vetting, selecting and beginning the migration to a new Student
  21| Information  System.    Technology  specialists  led  this  complex  effort,  moving  away  from  Aspen  X2  and  into
  22| Powerschool,  which  is  expected  to  be  a  more  user-friendly  platform,  serving
  23| students, parents and teachers more efficiently.  In addition, the school implemented
  24| Performance  Matters,  a  software  program  that  will  track  and  analyze  student
  25| outcomes.  By targeting student achievement and areas of concerns, teachers will be
  26| able to immediately address challenges students are facing and offer remediation.
  27| Across the school, students were provided with updated technology to improve
  28| vocational training.  The Business Technology program received new computers
  29| with  updated  software  for  their  students’         use.    Students  and  instructors  in  the
  30| school’s CAD/Drafting Technology program also received robust new computers
  31| and   associated   software,   and   the   school        ’s   busy   Graphic   Communications
  32| department  received  long       -awaited,  high  capacity  copiers,  so  that  their  older
  33| machines could be redistributed for teacher use throughout the campus.
  34| Finally, the school invested in a        Dell EMC Virtual server solution.  This advanced
  35| technology can handle the work of twenty             -one physical servers, placing them into
  36| four much more efficient nodes. These new nodes can handle up to seventy                     -five servers, so there is room for expansion
  37| as the school’s technology needs grow in future years.
  38| Monty Tech students continue to benefit from a 1:1 Chromebook initiative, as the school is in its 6                         th year of providing
  39| every student with their own device.  While the effects of the pandemic may have declined, lessons learned remain.
  40| Instructors who found that communicating with students through Google Classroom or Schoology continue to post
  41| lessons, collect assignments, and even offer assessments via this tool.  Throughout the 2021-2022 school year, the
  42| Technology Department continued to support the additional use of this technology, spending countless hours during the
  43| school year upgrading the network and increasing the bandwidth coming into the school to sustain video and audio
  44| conferencing, and increased network traffic.

   1|                               The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC) remains
   2|                               one of the school’s most worthwhile and engaging programs available to its students.  This
   3|                               outstanding leadership program, based on the foundations of instilling a value of citizenship,
   4|                               service to the United States, personal responsibility, and a sense of accomplishment, provides
   5|                               students with countless opportunities to serve as ambassadors in their communities, through the
   6|                               routine  demonstration  of  student  leadership  development,  volunteerism,  perseverance,  and
   7|                               dedication.
   8| During the 2021 - 2022 school year, MCJROTC Cadets participated in more than 2000 hours of community service and
   9| volunteerism.  Cadets assisted the Salvation Army by collecting more than $25,000 in donations, which provided aid to
  10| countless families in need during the holiday season.
  11| The Monty Tech MCJROTC cadets were actively involved in the Royalston Police Fair, providing assistance to local
  12| vendors,  the  police  department  and  community  residents  with  the  setup  and
  13| breakdown of this popular event.  This annual event raised more than $4,500 to
  14| support the Police Reform Act.
  15| Our cadets also enjoyed service at the Wachusett Brewery-Rock & Roll 5K in
  16| Westminster, MA, where they helped raise funds for the NEADS Organization.
  17| NEADS trains service dogs for individuals with mental and physical disabilities,
  18| many of whom are veterans.  To ensure this program was a success, cadets
  19| assisted with parking and traffic control for the runners, and assisted vendors with
  20| setup and tear down.
  21| Another community event that was a highlight for our students was the Annual
  22| Blacksmith Festival in Fitchburg MA. Monty Tech cadets assisted competitors
  23| and  vendors,  and  while  doing  so,  learned  a  great  deal  about  community
  24| volunteerism and the craft of blacksmithing as a fine art.
  25| Each year, we ask our school community to consider families who may not have a
  26| bountiful, healthy meal to look forward to during the holidays.  In 2021, we were
  27| humbled by an outpouring of donations, which allowed our cadets to assemble
  28| more  than  100  Thanksgiving  and  Christmas  baskets  to  be  distributed  to  deserving  families  in  the  Monty  Tech
  29| Community.
  30| While service to others and supporting community events are an essential part of our program, the Monty Tech cadets
  31| report a special feeling of pride when assisting with local color guards and participating in community parades.  In 2021-
  32| 2022, these special events were numerous, with as many as nine color guards local civic and veteran ceremonies, which
  33| are always a highlight for students and instructors alike.
  34| As our students learn the importance of giving back today, it is also important that they learn our history.  To that end,
  35| 150 cadets travelled to Boston to walk the freedom trail, visit Faneuil Hall and learn more about our American history.
  36| Finally, during the summer of 2022, 60 cadets participated in team building skills, leadership development and physical
  37| fitness at Prince William Forest in Triangle, VA.  They learned about the history of the Marine Corps as well as
  38| American flight history by visiting the Marine Corps Museum, and the Dulles Air and Space Museum.  Cadets also

   1| The Monty Tech School of Continuing Education had an exciting FY22, receiving more than $600,000 in training
   2| grants.  School leaders applied for and received a generous Career Technical Initiative Grant, which provided the
   3| funding needed to rollout new training programs, offered to qualified unemployed or underemployed participants at no
   4| charge.
   5| New training programs in Welding and HVAC were developed and saw their first cohort enroll.  Students completed 250
   6| hours of training, both in person and virtual, and left with industry-recognized credentials that include Hot Work
   7| Certification, OSHA 10 Hour Certification, and an American Welding Society (AWS) Endorsement. Upon completion,
   8| students were assisted, in partnership with MassHire North Central Career Center, in job placement.
   9| As we continue to monitor workforce needs and skills gaps, the School of Continuing Education works closely with the
  10| MassHire  North  Central  Career  Center  to  determine  specific  training  programs  that  are  most  in-demand.    Our
  11| overarching goal to support the North Central Massachusetts workforce readiness pipeline with an increase in career and
  12| licensure courses is evident in new programs that are developed, existing programs that are refined and in traditional
  13| journeyman/apprenticeship programs that remain a hallmark of our institution.
  14| Many other career-track students were eager to return in-person instruction. The popular Cosmetology program was able
  15| to  reopen  their  nighttime  salon,  providing  services  to  clientele  during  the
  16| evening hours, while electrical and plumbing programs were able to resume,
  17| and  students  were  back  in  the  classroom,  learning  from  experienced  and
  18| licensed professionals.
  19| A  large  medical  professional  shortage  arose  in  the  workforce,  due  to  the
  20| pandemic.  As a result, graduates from our evening medical programs continue
  21| to be in high demand.  Valuable training programs like Certified Phlebotomy
  22| Technician, Certified Clinical Medical Assistant, Certified EKG Technician,
  23| Certified Nurse Aide and Certified Home Health Aide, are able to help close
  24| noted workforce shortages. With state recognized and nationally recognized
  25| credentials, Monty Tech students are prepared and ready to join the regional
  26| workforce.
  27| Serving  more  than  1,000  community  members  annually,  the  Monty  Tech
  28| School of Continuing Education strives to provide an accessible and supportive
  29| learning environment to an adult population seeking workforce training that
  30| will lead to high-wage, high-skill jobs.
  31| The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely and ethically in a caring
  32| manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in diverse health care settings.
  33| On June 23, 2022 a graduating class of 16 students completed the Practical Nursing Program and entered the nursing
  34| profession. The class has thus far achieved a pass rate of 95% on the NCLEX-PN exam (National Council Licensure
  35| Examination for Practical Nurses).  Program graduates are currently employed in the health care profession throughout
  36| Massachusetts, working in various health care settings, such as long-term care, sub-acute care, mental health/substance
  37| abuse facilities, physicians’ offices and correctional medicine.
  38| The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to strengthen the                     “LPN to BSN (Bachelor of Science in Nursing)
  39| Bridge” relationship with Fitchburg State University (FSU).  Former graduates have successfully transferred into seats in
  40| the LPN to BSN Bridge program at FSU and will continue their education to a Bachelor’s of Science in Nursing.  Many
  41| class applicants cited their attraction to Monty Tech as the opportunity to complete a ten-month accelerated program and
  42| then bridge to Fitchburg State University. In the seven -year history of the Bridge Program, all of Monty Tech’s Practical
  43| Nursing graduates reaching graduation at FSU have passed their NCLEX-RN exam.  These former graduates have gone
  44| on to work in hospital settings including Heywood Hospital, Beth Israel, and Baystate Medical Center, while others have
  45| pursued  advanced  degrees  as  Critical  Care  Nurse  Practitioners,  Nurse  Educators,  and  Family  Practice  Nurse
  46| Practitioners.

   1|                                                 In 2021-2022, Monty Tech Practical Nursing students were asked to complete
   2|                                                 patient scenarios in the Sim Labs in Terms 2 and 3. The Faculty Sim team has
   3|                                                 implemented National League of Nursing (NLN) patient scenarios and authored
   4|                                                 several others consistent with the National Council of State Board of Nursing
   5|                                                 Detailed Test Plan.  Instructors have also utilized the Sim lab setting to instruct
   6|                                                 students in developing nursing skills that they may not be experiencing in the
   7|                                                 clinical setting.
   8|                                                  By  successfully  developing  and  implementing  a  vast  library  of  scenarios
   9|                                                 reflecting   International   Association   for   Clinical   Simulation   and   Learning
  10|                                                 standards,  we  hope  to  prepare  our  students  for  any  experience  they  may
  11|                                                 encounter as a newly licensed Practice Nurse.  In Maternity Sim Lab Boot Camp,
  12|                                                 for  example,  students  utilize  the  simulation  models  to  imitate  the  birthing
  13|                                                 process,  from  obstetric  office  visits  through  labor  and  common  postpartum
  14|                                                 complications. Well newborn care is also taught. Student feedback has been
  15| positive regarding these maternity scenarios, stating it better prepares them for an unexpected outcome during a delivery.
  16| This expanded content also better prepares students for Nursing Acceleration Challenge Exam success at Fitchburg State
  17| University.
  18| Substance abuse and the mental health setting job opportunities have increased with more funding being provided to
  19| combat the opiate crisis.       In addition to having specialty clinical rotations, students participate in simulation scenarios
  20| involving substance abuse and mental health clients, to help to prepare our graduates to work in a substance abuse
  21| clinical setting.
  22| The 2022-2023 school year may bring new leadership to Monty Tech, but the
  23| vision for improving programs and creative means to accomplish our collective
  24| goals  will  remain  intact.    In  any  given  year,  school  districts  may  make
  25| improvements to facilities, upgrade instructional materials, or even implement
  26| new and meaningful programs, but at Monty Tech, such evolution is expected.  It
  27| is imperative  that each of our twenty-one  vocational training programs  stays
  28| current,  and  that  our  training  facilities  are  safe  and  reflect  current  industry
  29| standards.    Local  budgets  do  not  always  allow  for  such  improvements  and
  30| innovation,  and  so  school  officials  continue  to  seek  funding  streams  and
  31| collaborative partnerships that allow the school to grow and expand to effectively
  32| serve students in grades 9-12 and beyond.  A few of the more notable initiatives
  33| that we expect to have a positive impact on our school and students in the coming
  34| years include:
  35| Farm-to-table instruction and increased access to healthy foods:  With a first-ever grant award from the Massachusetts
  36| Executive Office of Energy and Environmental Affairs, Monty Tech looks forward to the establishment of an on-site
  37| farm, housed in a shipping container.  The “Freight Farm” is expected to address negative economic impacts caused by
  38| the recent COVID crisis, still felt by our students and their families today.  With the capability of growing as many as
  39| 1,450 heads of lettuce every three weeks, this on-site farm will allow school officials to expand the school’s already
  40| generous meal program, saving the school as much as $38,000 in produce costs annually.  School officials look forward
  41| to using this incredible technology to infuse farm-to-table lessons into the Culinary Arts program, establish a Free Pantry
  42| at the School for any student in need, and grant free produce to students and families in need.
  43| Grant funding to improve and expand training programs:  Monty Tech has applied for and received eight Skills Capital
  44| Grants, totaling more than $2.5M.  School officials anticipate a FY23 application may be forthcoming, and as a result,
  45| have met to determine which program is in need of a shop renovation, updated equipment, and/or new technology that
  46| reflects current industry standards.  The school’s Culinary Arts program, which operates a busy, full-service restaurant,
  47| preparing students for both “front of the house” and “back of the house” careers may be the next vocational program to
  48| benefit from Skills Capital grant funding, should an application be approved. School officials envision improvements to
  49| the expansive training kitchen and restaurant area, to accommodate new and updated training equipment and industry
  50|                                                                         135

===PAGE 136=== (layout, 23 lines)
   1| trends.  In addition, because Monty Tech recently awarded a Round 5 Career Technical Initiative Grant, school officials
   2| look forward to expanding the  already successful evening training programs  to include  Electrical, Culinary Arts,
   3| Welding, Property Maintenance, and CNC Operator programs in FY23.
   4| New partnerships to increase access to vocational programs:  Since 2019, Monty Tech school officials have been in
   5| conversations with area school leaders, to determine if there is an interest in developing programs that would expand
   6| access to vocational training opportunities for students who may be enrolled in sending school districts, but who are
   7| looking for training that will support their post-graduate goals.  By modifying the Department of Elementary and
   8| Secondary Education’s “After Dark” guidelines, Monty Tech school officials are confident that a unique collaboration
   9| could provide just what these students seek.  In FY23, school officials expect to resume conversations, outlining plans to
  10| bring new training programs to area students.  Monty Tech will identify  grant opportunities to fund the needed
  11| equipment, supplies and materials, while partnering districts will work to develop student schedules and outline a
  12| Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) that outline’s their financial commitment to this endeavor.  While Monty Tech
  13| officials look forward to this work in 2022-2023, the highly anticipated training programs are not expected to open until
  14| Fall 2024, giving Monty Tech students and instructors ample time to locate a facility, and renovate it to create a safe,
  15| state-of-the-art training facility that replicates those already in operation on our main campus.
